ValuScope · model bake-off · October 11, 2026

Which model should write ValuScope's AI?

On ValuScope's current two-pass summary pipeline, Claude Opus, Fable and Sonnet scored about 8.8 out of 10; production's Gemini 3.6 Flash scored 5.8. Claude made roughly a quarter as many factual errors and was far better on the Q&A section. For comp sets, Gemini 3.1 Pro picks the best peers, but the bigger problem is the lookup that runs before any model: what users see today scored lower than every model tested.

8 models, Gemini and Claude6 real earnings calls, plus the prior quarter for each12 comp-set tickers2 blinded judges$11.68 API spend, plus subscription runs

The short version

Key Takeaways · recommended

Claude Opus 5.5

Scores 8.8 against 5.8 today, with the best accuracy (9.0), the fewest errors (0.7 per summary) and the best concision score of any model (8.8), staying the closest of the top three to the 900-word phone limit. It needs an Anthropic API key, an estimated 30 to 50 cents per summary (each call is summarised once), and maxDuration raised from 60 to 300, because its extraction pass likely takes 60 to 90 seconds.

Key Takeaways · alternatives

Sonnet 5.5, or Gemini 3.8 Flash

Sonnet scored 8.7 and had the best Q&A section, but ran to about 1,600 words, so the Claude judge marked it down for length. It costs about half as much as Opus. If you'd rather not add a vendor, Gemini 3.8 Flash is a one-line change: 6.4 against 5.8, every stage under 45 seconds, and cheaper than today.

Comp picker · recommended

Gemini 3.1 Pro

Scores 7.9 overall, and even Claude's judge ranks it level with Opus at the top. It's paid only, about 1 cent and 9 seconds a set, and picks are cached for 30 days. The free alternative is Gemini 3.8 Flash (6.2, against 5.4 today).

Fixes worth more than a model swap

Four pipeline bugs

The Q&A splitter misfires on 6 of 12 transcripts. "What changed" invents new and dropped topics for every model. The 60-second limit already times out production's Gemini on one call. And the comps lookup gives Toast six unrelated stocks and CrowdStrike no cybersecurity peers. Details below.

For each of six real calls, each model extracts this quarter's transcript and the prior quarter's, then writes the summary from those extractions alone, with quotes checked word for word against the transcript. Two judges, one from each vendor, read both transcripts and every summary blind and scored them 1 to 10.

Earlier round: the retired single-pass prompt

Before the two-pass pipeline shipped, summaries came from one prompt over the raw transcript. This round ran first, by mistake against that older prompt, but every model ran through the API there, so it holds the only like-for-like Claude timings and costs. It is a useful cross-check: the ranking held when the pipeline changed.

Findings

  • Gemini 3.6 Flash, today's model, is weakest where the new pipeline matters most. Its Q&A score was 5.1 against about 9 for the Claude models. Both judges said it marks nearly every answer "answered directly" and often leaves Unanswered empty, which defeats the purpose of the section. It also said Apple's CEO transition had already happened and labelled Tim Cook "Executive Chairman" throughout; the transcript has him speaking as CEO.
  • Production already runs into the 60-second limit. Gemini 3.6 Flash at its default thinking level took 75 seconds on Apple's prior-quarter extraction and 58 seconds on Chipotle's, and its median stage was 37 seconds. The limit is ValuScope's own maxDuration: 60 in vercel.json. With Fluid compute, which is on by default, the Hobby plan allows up to 300 seconds. Raising it is a one-line change that removes the risk for every model.
  • The Q&A splitter misfires on half the transcripts. segmentTranscript looks for phrases like "Q&A session" to find where questions begin. Deere's and Chipotle's opening legal notice ("Participants in the call, including the Q&A session, agree…") and Lilly's welcome trip it early. For Lilly the entire call became one 90-paragraph "exchange", and for Deere and Chipotle the prepared remarks were filed as exchange 1. That's 6 of the 12 transcripts. Every model except Haiku 4.5 repaired it themselves, splitting Lilly's call back into its 13 real questions, so the damage is mostly hidden today. But the pipeline is relying on the model to clean up after it, and the extraction prompt promises numbered exchanges it doesn't get.
  • "What changed" invents new and dropped topics for every model. The composer compares two short label lists from the extractions without seeing either transcript. So it lists things as "new" that were discussed last quarter (JPMorgan's cash tool, Kinsale's commercial auto) and as "dropped" things discussed at length this quarter. It was the most common error even for the best models. Working out new and dropped items in code, or checking each one against the prior transcript, would fix it for any model.
  • The judges agree on the order but not on length. Gemini's judge gave Sonnet its top score (9.7), while Claude's judge gave it 7.7, citing summaries that ran about 1,600 words against the 900-word phone limit. Opus trimmed its own drafts toward the limit and scored 8.8 on concision, the highest of any model. Both judges put all three of Opus, Fable and Sonnet clearly above every Gemini model.
  • Haiku: two very different results. The subscription only offers Haiku 4.5, which came last on the new pipeline (3.0): it marked nearly every answer direct, misattributed quotes and invented dropped topics. Haiku 5.5, tested by API in the earlier round, scored 7.4 there and beat every Gemini model. It's probably the cheapest good option, but it hasn't been tested on the current pipeline.
  • No model made up numbers in the earlier round. The figure check found every number in all 48 single-pass summaries in its transcript except one sum (24 + 176 broker appointments written as 200). The errors that remain are subtler: wrong attribution, reversed cause and effect, hedges turned into commitments, and the wrong period.
  • Sonnet 5.5 broke on comp picks. At low effort it returned two peers for Celsius plus Celsius itself, and for Hims it returned three peers and a literal "HIMS_PLACEHOLDER". It's strong at summaries and unreliable as a comp picker, so the two features shouldn't share a model just because one is good.
  • Every model suggests dead tickers. Between 6% and 11% of comp suggestions were dead or recycled symbols: SQ (Block is now XYZ), CYBR (bought by Palo Alto), UDMY, FI and OLO. Only Claude Opus 5.5 knew Block's new ticker. The live-quote check in peers.js is essential; keep it whatever model runs.
  • Two production comps bugs. CrowdStrike's curated set is MSFT, AMZN, GOOGL, SNOW, MDB, DDOG, NET and NVDA. getCuratedComps merges CRWD's three groups in table order and stops at eight before it reaches Cybersecurity. Toast's similar-stocks lookup returns IOT, GTLB, MDB, DOCN, RBRK and MNDY, which share Yahoo's "Software - Infrastructure" label but have nothing to do with restaurant payments. Cava's set is padded with On Running.
  • Gemini 3.1 Pro rates its own comp sets highly. Its judge gave its own sets 9.0, while Claude's judge gave them 6.8. Even on Claude's judge alone it ties Opus for first, so the pick stands, but the real margin is smaller than the overall column suggests.
  • Method notes. The Anthropic key ran out of credit partway through. On the current pipeline, the four Claude models and the Claude judge ran as Claude Code subagents on your subscription. Every pass was a separate subagent given the exact system prompt, input and schema, and the composer never saw the transcript. Their answers went back through the real callSummary.js, including quote verification. Subagents run at Claude Code's default effort, so these Claude results may be somewhat better, longer and slower than the low-effort API setting you would deploy. The cost and time estimates for Claude come from output size and the API speeds measured in the earlier round. Gemini ran through the real API throughout. Each model ran once per input, so per-call scores are noisy and the averages are what count.

The slowest single pass for each Gemini model in the current pipeline. Production runs every pass as its own Vercel request with a 60-second limit, so one slow pass means a failed summary, not just a slow one.

How each output was scored

AccuracyEvery figure, attribution and claim checked against the transcript. Wrong numbers, wrong people, wrong periods and invented content pull it down hard.
Q&AThe exchanges chosen carry signal, are attributed to the right people, and the "read" on how directly each was answered is fair.
CoverageResults versus prior periods, guidance changes, strategic news, what analysts pressed on.
InsightSurfaces the surprise, the number that moved or the question management avoided, and a "what changed" that is real.
ConcisionTight and scannable on a phone, within about 900 words, no filler or repetition.
DisciplineStatements attributed by name and title, numbers with every claim, no advice, no outside knowledge.
Comp relevance, coverage, scale, noiseSame business model and economics; obvious competitors present; sensible size; no misfits or wrong tickers.

Judges: Claude Opus 5.5 and Gemini 3.1 Pro, list order shuffled differently for each judge. Gemini 3.1 Pro judged through the API at high reasoning effort throughout. Claude Opus 5.5 judged through the API until the credit ran out, then as a subagent given the identical blinded prompt and nothing else. On the current pipeline the judges also saw the prior quarter's transcript, to check "what changed". Gemini 3.1 Pro and Claude Opus 5.5 are also contestants, so each judge marked its own output among the others without knowing which it was. In the earlier round, a separate figure check pulled every number out of each summary and looked for it in the transcript, including spoken forms like "two and a half"; across all 48 summaries only 1 figure had no source within rounding.

Mega-cap, heavily scripted call
AAPL · fiscal Q3 2026 call, reported 2026-07-30 · 108 transcript paragraphs

Claude Fable 5.19.5

Apple's June quarter revenue rose 16% to a record $109.4B, with iPhone up 22% to $54.3B and Mac up 29% to $10.4B despite supply constraints; diluted EPS rose 29% to $2.02 including $0.11 of tariff refunds. Kevan Parekh, CFO, guided September revenue growth to 9%-11%, down from the 14%-17% June guide, on a roughly 2.5-point FX headwind and supply constraints expected to increase significantly. Tim Cook, CEO, said this was his final earnings call.

Key takeaways

  • Tim Cook, CEO, blamed the advanced-node SoC constraint on demand running ahead of forecast, not suppliers, with constraints broadening from Mac to iPhone, Mac and iPad in September.
  • Cook said Apple reluctantly raised iPad and Mac prices amid a "100-year flood" in memory pricing, with costs higher again in September.
  • Services growth slowed from 16% to 12%, which Kevan Parekh, CFO, attributed mainly to FX plus the F1 compare, mobile gaming softness and the U.S. link-out ruling.
  • Siri AI arrives this fall; Cook said Apple has no complete plan yet for recovering compute costs beyond iCloud+ upgrade tiers, and cannot yet launch it on iPhone and iPad in the EU.

Key metrics

  • Revenue $109.4B +16% YoY
  • iPhone revenue $54.3B +22% YoY
  • Mac revenue $10.4B +29% YoY
  • Services revenue $30.7B +12% YoY, from 16%
  • Gross margin 50.1% 48.1% ex refunds vs 49.3%
  • Diluted EPS $2.02 +29% YoY
  • Operating cash flow $34.4B June quarter record

Financial highlights

  • Revenue $109.4B (+16%) with June quarter records in every geographic segment; products $78.7B (+18%), services $30.7B (+12%).
  • iPhone $54.3B (+22%), Mac $10.4B (+29%), iPad $6.2B (-6% on the A16 iPad compare), Wearables $7.9B (+6%).
  • Gross margin 50.1% including about 2 points of tariff refunds; 48.1% excluding them versus 49.3% in March, a decline Parekh said memory costs more than explain.
  • Net income $29.8B, EPS $2.02 and operating cash flow $34.4B were June quarter records even excluding refunds; OpEx $19.1B (+23%); $33B returned to shareholders.

Guidance

  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, expects September revenue growth of 9%-11% (June guide 14%-17%), with FX a roughly 2.5-point sequential headwind and constraints across iPhone, Mac and iPad.
  • Parekh expects mid-teens iPhone growth and services growth similar to June's 12% after a further 2.5-point FX headwind.
  • Parekh guided gross margin to 47%-48% including about 1 point of tariff refunds (46.5% ex-refund versus 48.1%), the decline more than explained by memory.
  • Parekh guided OpEx to $19.1B-$19.4B, OI&E around $350M and tax around 16.5%; no color beyond September.

Q&A

  • Amit Daryanani, Evercore: How much of the roughly 500 bps September deceleration is supply versus FX, and why are constraints broadening?
    Kevan Parekh, CFO: FX is a roughly 2.5-point sequential headwind and supply constraints increase significantly across iPhone, Mac and iPad, together bridging to June's growth. Tim Cook, CEO: memory costs rose significantly from March to June, will be higher in September, and the carry-in offset shrinks after that.
    Answered partially; no supply-versus-FX split.
  • Ben Reitzes, Melius Research: How much do supply constraints hit the December quarter, and could Apple broaden its silicon suppliers?
    Tim Cook, CEO: The root cause is a demand forecast miss, with iPhone up 22% and Mac up 29%, and September will be "scrambling on the supply side"; Parekh gave no guidance beyond September. Cook: "this isn't a partner or supplier issue"; Apple sources over 100M Arizona components this year.
    Declined to quantify December; pushed back on supplier premise.
  • Erik Woodring, Morgan Stanley: Will Apple pursue multi-year supplier LTAs, protect gross profit dollars or margins, and why did services decelerate?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Pricing weighs units, revenue and margin over the long term, not a 90-day clock; LTAs went unaddressed. Parekh: services fell from 16% to 12% mainly on FX, plus the F1 compare, mobile gaming and the link-out ruling.
    Answered partially; LTAs not addressed.
  • Samik Chatterjee, JPMorgan: Where does Siri AI stand in China and Europe, and how much of the gross margin decline is FX versus commodities?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Apple is working with the EU commission and offers Siri AI on Mac there; China approved original Apple Intelligence features last week. Parekh: the 120 bps drop to 48.1% and the 160 bps drop to 46.5% are each more than explained by memory, with FX "a pretty minimal impact".
    Answered directly.

Unanswered

  • Ben Reitzes, Melius Research: How much do supply constraints hit the December quarter? Kevan Parekh, CFO: "Beyond September, we're not providing any color at this point."
  • Michael Ng, Goldman Sachs: What adoption does Apple expect for Apple Upgrade, and will it shorten the replacement cycle? Tim Cook, CEO, said uptake remains to be seen; no figures given.
  • Wamsi Mohan, Bank of America: How is the competitive landscape changing with potential OpenAI or SpaceX AI devices? John Ternus, incoming CEO, said Apple is focused on its own plans; no comment on competitors.

Notable quotes

  • “We did it because we're in what I would characterize as a 100-year flood on the memory pricing, with exponential increases in memory prices.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “We've got a quarter that we're going to be scrambling on the supply side, essentially.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “Let me stress again, this isn't a partner or supplier issue.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “Beyond September, we're not providing any color at this point.” Kevan Parekh, CFO

What changed

  • Guidance: Revenue growth: 14%-17% to 9%-11%.
  • Guidance: Gross margin: 47.5%-48.5% to 47%-48%, now including about 1 point of tariff refunds.
  • Guidance: OpEx: $18.8B-$19.1B to $19.1B-$19.4B; OI&E: $250M to $350M; tax: 17% to 16.5%.
  • Guidance: FX: 2.5-point tailwind to roughly 2.5-point headwind.
  • Guidance: Supply constraints: Mac Mini, Mac Studio and MacBook Neo, to iPhone, Mac and iPad.
  • New mentions: Siri AI and iOS 27 public beta, Apple Upgrade leasing program with Klarna, A19 and A19 Pro chips, Broadcom agreement exceeding $30B, iPad and Mac price increases, Ask to Browse, Time Allowances and iCloud+ upgrade tiers
  • Dropped: Net cash neutral framework change and $100B buyback, Google collaboration on foundational models, Apple Maps advertising, India opportunity and Greater China growth detail
  • Tone: Q2 was expansive about demand ("off the charts", "over the moon excited about India"); Q3 turned defensive, with Tim Cook citing a "100-year flood" in memory and "scrambling on the supply side" while still insisting the constraint is demand-driven.

Risks mentioned

  • Supply constraints on advanced-node SoCs, which Tim Cook, CEO, called "very significant" with limited flexibility to remedy.
  • Memory costs rising again in September and, per Cook, continuing to increase beyond it as the carry-in inventory offset shrinks.
  • FX: about 2.5 points sequential on total growth and about 5 points cumulative on services from March to September, per Kevan Parekh, CFO.
  • App Store headwinds from mobile gaming, business model changes and the U.S. link-out ruling; unknown price elasticity; Siri AI regulatory delays in the EU and China.
Claude Opus 5.5 · 9.0

The best balance of density and accuracy: correct attributions, precise margin and FX walks, a fair unanswered list and a concrete tone shift.

  • Lists 'A19 and A19 Pro chips' as a new mention; the prior-quarter call already mentioned A19 and A19 Pro.
  • 'Siri AI arrives this fall' is not stated; Cook's 'this fall' referred to the child-safety features and OS updates.
Gemini 3.1 Pro · 10.0

Flawless summary. Highly concise, accurate, and insightful, capturing the tone shift and the specific drivers of the margin and revenue guidance.

Subscription subagent · 1194 words · 4 verified quotes

Claude Sonnet 5.59.0

Apple's fiscal Q3 2026 revenue rose 16% to $109.4B, with iPhone up 22% to $54.3B and Mac up 29% to $10.4B, on Tim Cook's final earnings call as CEO. Kevan Parekh, CFO, guided September-quarter revenue growth to 9%-11% on supply constraints and FX, and gross margin to 47%-48% as memory costs rise. Diluted EPS was $2.02, up 29%, including $0.11 from tariff refunds.

Key takeaways

  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, guided September revenue growth to 9%-11%, down from 16% in June, citing a 2.5-point sequential FX headwind and supply constraints that increase significantly across iPhone, Mac and iPad.
  • Tim Cook, CEO, said the root cause of the constraints is a demand-forecast miss, with advanced-node SoC availability the primary limit and iPhone (22% growth) and Mac (29%) far above expectations.
  • Cook said Apple 'reluctantly' raised prices amid memory pricing he called a '100-year flood', and has now raised prices on iPad and Mac.
  • Parekh said services growth slowed from 16% in March to 12% in June, mainly on FX, no F1 theatrical release this year, and App Store softness in mobile gaming.
  • Cook said Siri AI developer and public beta feedback has been overwhelmingly positive, while China approved the original Apple Intelligence features last week and Siri AI there needs more work.

Key metrics

  • Revenue $109.4B up 16% y/y; June quarter record
  • iPhone revenue $54.3B up 22% y/y; June quarter record
  • Mac revenue $10.4B up 29% y/y; June quarter record
  • iPad revenue $6.2B down 6% y/y
  • Services revenue $30.7B up 12% y/y; June quarter record
  • Company gross margin 50.1% up 80bp sequentially; about 2 points from tariff refunds
  • Diluted EPS $2.02 up 29% y/y; includes $0.11 tariff refund
  • Cash and marketable securities $147B total debt $84B

Financial highlights

  • Revenue was $109.4B, up 16%, with Products at $78.7B (up 18%) and Services at $30.7B (up 12%).
  • Company gross margin was 50.1%, up 80bp sequentially, including about 2 points of tariff refund benefit; Parekh said it was 48.1% excluding the refund.
  • Operating expenses were $19.1B, up 23%, and net income was $29.8B.
  • Diluted EPS was $2.02, up 29%, including a $0.11 favorable tariff refund impact.
  • Apple generated $34.4B of operating cash flow and returned $33B, including $25.8B of share repurchases and $4B of dividends and equivalents.

Guidance

  • Parekh guided September-quarter total revenue growth of 9%-11%, with FX a 2.5-point sequential headwind to y/y growth from June.
  • Parekh expects supply constraints to increase significantly sequentially and to affect iPhone, Mac and iPad, and guided reported iPhone growth of mid-teens.
  • Parekh expects reported services growth largely similar to June's 12% after removing the negative sequential FX impact of about 2.5 points.
  • Parekh guided gross margin of 47%-48%, including about 1 point of tariff refund benefit, operating expenses of $19.1B-$19.4B, other income and expense of about $350M and a tax rate of about 16.5%.
  • Cook expects higher memory costs in September, partly offset by carry-in inventory and lower non-memory costs, with market memory pricing continuing to rise beyond September; Parekh gave no guidance beyond the September quarter.

Q&A

  • Amit Daryanani, Evercore: How much of the roughly 500bp deceleration implied by September guidance is supply versus FX, which constraints are broadening beyond advanced SoCs, and is memory sourcing flexibility about supply, cost or customer value?
    Kevan Parekh, CFO: Parekh said September growth reflects about 2.5 points of FX headwind plus supply constraints that, together, get close to June's total growth rate. Cook said the constraints are 'very significant' with limited flexibility, memory cost rose in June versus March and will rise again in September, and the carry-in inventory benefit shrinks beyond September. Cook said DRAM has three suppliers and Apple is 'evaluating all options'.
    Answered partially: gave the FX and cost direction but no split of the deceleration between supply and FX.
  • Ben Reitzes, Melius Research: How much do supply constraints hit the December quarter, is there guidance beyond September, and could Apple broaden its silicon providers quickly?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Cook said the root cause is a demand-forecast miss, with iPhone up 22% and Mac up 29%, and September will be a quarter of scrambling on supply. Kevan Parekh, CFO, gave no color beyond September. Cook said it is not a partner or supplier issue and that Apple sources over 100 million components this year from Arizona under its $600B U.S. commitment.
    Answered partially: declined December guidance and did not say whether providers would be broadened.
  • Erik Woodring, Morgan Stanley: Is Apple pursuing multi-year LTAs, is pricing aimed at gross profit dollars or margin percentage, and why does services growth of 12% decelerate further?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Cook said Apple reluctantly raised prices and weighs units, revenue and margin without a formula. Kevan Parekh, CFO, said services growth fell from 16% to 12% on FX, no F1 release and App Store softness in mobile gaming, with FX a 5-point headwind to services y/y growth from March to September.
    Answered partially: services was explained with numbers, but LTAs and the dollars-versus-percentage question were not.
  • Aaron Rakers, Wells Fargo: Has Apple seen demand pull-forward given memory pricing, and does edge AI open new addressable markets?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Cook said iPhone has run about 22% growth year to date and pull-forward is not obvious in the data. He said it is too early to judge price elasticity after the iPad and Mac price increases, and called on-device AI a strategic competitive weapon.
    Answered partially: pushed back on the premise and named no new markets.
  • Michael Ng, Goldman Sachs: What adoption is expected for Apple Upgrade, and what do the iOS 27 betas say about Siri AI as a holiday driver and about compute costs?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Cook said Apple Upgrade is a leasing plan offered in retail stores and uptake is still to be seen. Kevan Parekh, CFO, added it is U.S.-only for now. Cook said he does not claim a complete plan for compute costs, with iCloud+ upgrades possible for heavy users.
    Answered partially: no view on holiday demand or replacement cycles.
  • Samik Chatterjee, JPMorgan: What are the hurdles for Siri AI in China and Europe, and what drives gross margin from 48% in June to about 46.5% in September?
    Kevan Parekh, CFO: Parekh said gross margin fell 120bp from 49.3% in March to 48.1% in June ex-refund, and a further 160bp to the 46.5% guidance midpoint, with memory cost explaining more than 100% of each move and FX minimal. Cook said China approved the original Apple Intelligence features last week, and Siri AI is or will be offered on Mac in the EU.
    Answered directly on margin; Siri AI timing in China and the EU was general.

Unanswered

  • Ben Reitzes, Melius Research: Could Apple broaden its silicon providers within a reasonable time to ease supply constraints? Tim Cook, CEO, said it is not a partner or supplier issue and cited Arizona sourcing, without saying whether providers would be broadened.
  • Erik Woodring, Morgan Stanley: Is Apple pursuing multi-year LTAs, and is pricing meant to protect gross profit dollars or margin percentage? Cook said there is no formula and weighed units, revenue and margin; LTAs were not addressed and no target was given.
  • Michael Ng, Goldman Sachs: Will Apple Upgrade shorten replacement cycles, and is Siri AI a holiday iPhone demand driver? Cook said uptake is still to be seen and feedback is positive; holiday demand and replacement-cycle effects were not addressed.
  • Wamsi Mohan, Bank of America: Will Siri AI change Apple's capital intensity, and how does John Ternus view AI-device entrants such as OpenAI and SpaceX AI? Cook said the cost impact is to be seen; John Ternus, Incoming CEO, spoke of opportunity and Apple's plans without addressing the named competitors.

Notable quotes

  • “We're seeing some very significant constraints currently, with limited flexibility in the supply chain to remedy it.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “On the pricing front, we reluctantly raised prices, I would say.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “It's not obvious in the data that what you're asking is true.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “I don't want to say that we have a complete plan for that.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “I've never been more confident that the best is yet to come.” Tim Cook, CEO

What changed

  • Guidance: Revenue growth guide moved from 14%-17% for June (actual 16%) to 9%-11% for September.
  • Guidance: Gross margin guide moved from 47.5%-48.5% to 47%-48%, now including about 1 point of tariff refund benefit.
  • Guidance: Operating expenses guide moved from $18.8B-$19.1B to $19.1B-$19.4B.
  • Guidance: Other income and expense guide moved from about $250M to about $350M, and tax rate from about 17% to about 16.5%.
  • Guidance: Services growth guide moved from similar to March's 16% to largely similar to June's 12%, both after removing FX.
  • New mentions: Siri AI, Apple Upgrade (with Klarna), iOS 27 public beta, iPhone Air, Broadcom agreement (>$30 billion), Ask to Browse and Time Allowances, Apple Sports, Creator Studio
  • Dropped: Capital allocation (net cash neutral dropped, $100 billion buyback), China and India growth, Google collaboration on foundation models
  • Tone: The prior call centered on demand outrunning supply for Mac and a new capital return framework; this call centers on broader supply constraints, memory-driven margin compression and price increases, with Tim Cook, CEO, using phrases such as 'scrambling' and 'reluctantly raised prices'.

Risks mentioned

  • Supply constraints from advanced-node SoC availability are expected to increase significantly in the September quarter across iPhone, Mac and iPad.
  • Memory costs rose in June and are expected to rise further in September, with market pricing increasing beyond September and the carry-in inventory benefit shrinking.
  • FX is a 2.5-point sequential headwind to total company growth, and about 5 points to services growth from March to September.
  • A court ruling on U.S. link-out transactions remains in effect while the Supreme Court hears Apple's appeal.
  • Price elasticity after the iPad and Mac price increases is not yet known, and Siri AI compute costs lack a complete recovery plan.

Gemini 3.8 Flash7.0

Apple reported fiscal Q3 2026 revenue of $109.4 billion, up 16% year-over-year, driven by record June quarter revenue in iPhone, Mac, and Services. Diluted earnings per share increased 29% to $2.02, supported by a 50.1% gross margin that included approximately two percentage points of benefit from tariff refunds. The call marked Tim Cook's final earnings call as CEO ahead of handing leadership to incoming CEO John Ternus.

Key takeaways

  • Revenue grew 16% year-over-year to a June quarter record of $109.4 billion, led by a 22% increase in iPhone revenue to $54.3 billion and 29% growth in Mac revenue.
  • Gross margin reached 50.1%, aided by roughly two percentage points from tariff refunds, but Kevan Parekh, CFO, expects gross margins to moderate to 47% to 48% in Q4.
  • Supply constraints are expanding into Q4 to affect iPhone, Mac, and iPad, which Tim Cook, CEO, attributed to demand outpacing company forecasts rather than supplier failure.
  • Tim Cook, CEO, noted the company reluctantly raised prices to counter exponential memory price increases, which he characterized as a 100-year flood.
  • Tim Cook, CEO, confirmed this was his final earnings call, with incoming CEO John Ternus taking over earnings calls going forward.

Key metrics

  • Total Revenue $109.4 billion Up 16% year-over-year
  • Diluted EPS $2.02 Up 29% year-over-year
  • Gross Margin 50.1% Up 80 basis points sequentially
  • iPhone Revenue $54.3 billion Up 22% year-over-year
  • Mac Revenue $10.4 billion Up 29% year-over-year
  • Services Revenue $30.7 billion Up 12% year-over-year
  • Operating Cash Flow $34.4 billion June quarter record
  • Active Installed Base Over 2.5 billion devices All-time high

Financial highlights

  • Total revenue reached $109.4 billion, an increase of 16% year-over-year.
  • Net income reached a June quarter record of $29.8 billion, generating diluted earnings per share of $2.02, up 29% year-over-year.
  • Products revenue climbed 18% year-over-year to $78.7 billion, while Services revenue rose 12% to a record $30.7 billion.
  • Operating cash flow reached a June quarter record of $34.4 billion, and Apple returned $33 billion to shareholders during the quarter.
  • Paid subscriptions crossed 1.5 billion, and the active installed base reached an all-time high of over 2.5 billion devices.

Guidance

  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, expects Q4 FY2026 total revenue growth of 9% to 11% year-over-year, reflecting a sequential foreign exchange headwind of about 2.5 percentage points.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, projects mid-teens year-over-year revenue growth for iPhone in Q4 FY2026.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, expects Services revenue growth in Q4 FY2026 to track similarly to Q3's 12% before absorbing an approximate 2.5 percentage point sequential foreign exchange drag.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, targets Q4 FY2026 gross margin between 47% and 48%, which includes approximately one percentage point of tariff refund benefit.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, forecasts Q4 FY2026 operating expenses between $19.1 billion and $19.4 billion, and an effective tax rate of around 16.5%.

Q&A

  • Amit Daryanani, Evercore: How much of the Q4 revenue deceleration to 9%-11% is due to supply constraints versus foreign exchange, what are the constraints, and will DRAM sourcing flexibility mitigate costs?
    Kevan Parekh, CFO: The deceleration from June is driven by an approximate 2.5 percentage point sequential foreign exchange drag and significantly widening supply constraints across iPhone, Mac, and iPad, while memory cost inflation will rise further in September despite inventory offsets.
    answered directly
  • Michael Ng, Goldman Sachs: What are the adoption expectations for the Apple Upgrade Program, and what are the learnings from the Siri AI beta on compute costs and monetization?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Apple Upgrade remains limited to U.S. retail stores, and while compute cost planning is early, Apple expects heavy Siri AI users will have options to migrate to higher-tier iCloud+ subscriptions.
    answered partially
  • Ben Reitzes, Melius Research: How significantly will supply constraints impact growth in the December quarter, and will Apple broaden silicon sourcing using the Arizona fab?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Apple declined to provide guidance beyond September, attributing current supply tightness to demand exceeding forecasts, while noting the Arizona fab is sourcing over 100 million components this year.
    answered partially
  • Erik Woodring, Morgan Stanley: What is the pricing philosophy regarding gross profit dollars versus margins, and what factors explain the deceleration in Services growth?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Price adjustments were made reluctantly to manage exponential memory cost spikes holistically across volume and margin, while Services faced foreign exchange headwinds of 2.5 percentage points, mobile gaming softness, link-out transaction rulings, and tough theatrical compares.
    answered directly
  • Samik Chatterjee, JPMorgan: What regulatory hurdles remain for Siri AI in China and the EU, and how much of the sequential gross margin drop to 46.5% ex-tariffs is memory versus foreign exchange?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Siri AI will ship on Mac in the EU while working with regulators, China approved initial Apple Intelligence features, and more than 100% of the adjusted 160 basis point sequential gross margin drop is caused by memory cost inflation.
    answered directly

Unanswered

  • Michael Ng, Goldman Sachs: What are the expected adoption rates for the Apple Upgrade Program across the install base and its impact on replacement cycles? Tim Cook described the leasing mechanics and stated it is currently only available in U.S. retail stores, avoiding adoption metrics or replacement cycle impacts.
  • Ben Reitzes, Melius Research: How significantly will supply constraints impact the December quarter growth? Tim Cook explicitly declined to comment on or provide guidance for periods beyond the September quarter.
  • Wamsi Mohan, Bank of America: Will Siri AI increase Apple's capital intensity, and how is the competitive landscape evolving against competitor AI devices? Tim Cook described the hybrid data center model and cost buckets without quantifying capital intensity changes, while John Ternus offered only general excitement about Apple's roadmap.

Notable quotes

  • “As you know, this will be my final earnings call, and John will lead these calls going forward.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “We've got a quarter that we're going to be scrambling on the supply side, essentially.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “We reluctantly raised prices, I would say.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “We did it because we're in what I would characterize as a 100-year flood on the memory pricing, with exponential increases in memory prices.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “The root cause of it is not a regular supply issue. It's a demand forecast issue, to be candid,” Tim Cook, CEO

What changed

  • Guidance: Total company revenue growth guidance moved from 14% to 17% for Q3 FY2026 to 9% to 11% for Q4 FY2026.
  • Guidance: Gross margin guidance moved from 47.5% to 48.5% for Q3 FY2026 to 47% to 48% (inclusive of ~1 percentage point tariff refund) for Q4 FY2026.
  • Guidance: Operating expenses guidance moved from $18.8 billion to $19.1 billion for Q3 FY2026 to $19.1 billion to $19.4 billion for Q4 FY2026.
  • Guidance: Other income and expense guidance moved from around $250 million for Q3 FY2026 to around $350 million for Q4 FY2026.
  • Guidance: Effective tax rate guidance moved from around 17% for Q3 FY2026 to around 16.5% for Q4 FY2026.
  • New mentions: Siri AI, Klarna, Broadcom agreement, Apple Advanced Manufacturing Center, Ask to Browse, Time Allowances, Accessibility Reader, Creator Studio, Widow's Bay
  • Dropped: Evaluation of cash and debt independence following the dropped net cash neutral target, $100 billion share repurchase authorization, Apple Maps search and discovery ads, Greater China and India specific regional growth metrics
  • Tone: The tone shifted from broad product optimism to an urgent focus on cost inflation and production constraints, with Tim Cook explicitly stating the company would be scrambling on supply due to demand outstripping forecasts and navigating a 100-year flood in memory pricing.

Risks mentioned

  • Severe memory pricing inflation, described as a 100-year flood, which drives more than 100% of the sequential gross margin contraction going into Q4.
  • Widespread hardware supply constraints sequentially expanding to limit iPhone, Mac, and iPad availability due to demand exceeding forecasts.
  • Foreign exchange volatility creating an approximate 2.5 percentage point sequential headwind to year-over-year revenue growth in Q4, and a 5 percentage point headwind for Services from March to September.
  • Regulatory hurdles delaying Siri AI rollouts in the European Union and China, alongside link-out transaction rulings impacting Services.
  • Softness in mobile gaming pressuring Services segment growth.

Claude Sonnet 5.59.0

JPMorgan reported fiscal Q2 2026 net income of $16.9B, EPS of $6.14 and ROTCE of 23%, with revenue ex significant items up 15% y/y and expenses up 15% to $27.3B. Corporate and Investment Bank revenue rose 27% to $24.9B on equities up 86% and investment banking fees up 30%. Jeremy Barnum, CFO, raised full-year NII ex-Markets guidance to about $96.5B and expense guidance to about $107.5B.

Key takeaways

  • Equities markets revenue rose 86% y/y and IB fees rose 30%, though Jeremy Barnum, CFO, said there was pull forward and that this quarter's equities set of events is hard to imagine repeating.
  • Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO, said 'It's getting close to as good as it gets' but that nobody knows how long it lasts.
  • Dimon said the board named two co-presidents to prepare them to do far more, the CEO timetable is essentially unchanged at several years, plus or minus, and the date is up to the board.
  • Dimon said AI has almost 1,000 use cases, about 50 really important, with some discrete areas cutting jobs 30% or 40%, and that the benefits will accrue to customers rather than margins.
  • Dimon said he made a mistake mentioning $20B of buybacks last time, and that the goal is deploying capital at a 17% return, organic growth first.

Key metrics

  • Net income $16.9B
  • EPS $6.14
  • ROTCE 23%
  • Revenue ex significant items up 15% y/y
  • Expenses $27.3B up 15% y/y
  • Standardized CET1 ratio 14.1% down 20bp q/q
  • CIB revenue $24.9B up 27% y/y
  • Equities markets revenue up 86% y/y fixed income markets up 6%

Financial highlights

  • Net income was $16.9B and EPS was $6.14, with ROTCE of 23% and revenue ex significant items up 15% y/y.
  • Expenses were $27.3B, up 15% y/y, and credit costs were $2.5B, with net charge-offs of $2.4B and a net reserve build of $149M.
  • Standardized CET1 was 14.1%, down 20bp q/q, with standardized RWA up about $103B.
  • CIB net income was $9.7B on revenue of $24.9B, up 27%, with fixed income markets revenue up 6%.
  • AWM revenue was $6.9B, up 19%, with a 38% pre-tax margin and $50B of long-term net inflows; CCB revenue was $20.3B, up 8%.

Guidance

  • Barnum guided full-year 2026 NII ex-Markets to about $96.5B, up from $95B, with total NII of about $105.5B and Markets NII of about $9B.
  • Barnum guided adjusted expense to about $107.5B, raised by $2.5B, with $1.5B booked in the first half and about $1B implied for the second half.
  • Barnum guided the full-year card net charge-off rate to approximately 3.2%, versus approximately 3.4% last quarter.
  • Barnum said consumer deposits are still expected to grow low single digits this year, and the board intends to raise the quarterly dividend to $1.65 per share effective Q3.
  • Barnum said AI token expense was trivial in the first half and is forecast to accelerate meaningfully in the second half, though still trivial for the full year.

  • Ken Usdin, Autonomous Research: What should investors take from the co-president appointments, and how sustainable are investment banking and markets activity?
    Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO: Dimon said the board made two co-presidents to prepare them to do far more and the timetable has not changed. Jeremy Barnum, CFO, said IB fees were not super-peak, with some pull forward and large IPOs contributing, the pipeline is quite robust, and he declined to guide. Barnum said the equities events this quarter are hard to imagine repeating.
    Answered directly on succession; declined to guide on investment banking.
  • John McDonald, Truist Securities: What drove the upward revision to NII ex-Markets, and why is Markets NII guided higher?
    Jeremy Barnum, CFO: Barnum said NII ex-Markets moved from $95B to $96.5B, driven mainly by deposit balances across wholesale and consumer plus a mix shift toward slightly higher margin, with consumer betas outperforming the model. He said Markets NII rises on lower non-interest-bearing financing assets in the second half, and raising CIB equity allocation shifts about $150M of NII into Markets NII.
    Answered directly.
  • Erika Najarian, UBS: What is the board looking for in the next CEO, and is Dimon's remaining tenure fixed or rolling?

  • Barnum said deposit rate-paid convexity has not shown up yet but must kick in at some point, and a 7% rate environment would not be great.

JPMorgan Chase earned $16.9B, or $6.14 per share, in Q2 2026 with a 23% ROTCE, as equities markets revenue rose 86% and IB fees 30%. Jeremy Barnum, CFO, raised NII ex-Markets guidance to ~$96.5B from $95B and adjusted expense guidance to ~$107.5B, while Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO, said conditions are "getting close to as good as it gets."

  • Barnum said this quarter's equities drivers (major IPOs, index rebalancing, Korean market dynamics) are statistically improbable to repeat.
  • Dimon elevated two co-presidents and said his succession timetable is unchanged at "several years" or "a few years," up to the board.
  • Barnum put excess capital at ~$40B to deploy over time, and Dimon set a 17% return goal on deployed capital, open to adjacency M&A but organic first.

Gemini 3.6 FlashProduction5.5

JPMorgan Chase reported Q2 2026 net income of $16.9 billion and diluted EPS of $6.14, with revenue ex-significant items rising 15% year-on-year. Chairman and CEO Jamie Dimon noted operating conditions are close to as good as it gets, while Chief Financial Officer Jeremy Barnum reported higher wholesale deposits and raised full-year guidance for NII ex-Markets to about $96.5 billion and adjusted expenses to about $107.5 billion.

Key takeaways

  • Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO, announced the elevation of Doug and Troy to co-presidents and clarified that CEO succession timing remains unchanged at a few years plus or minus.
  • Jeremy Barnum, CFO, reported robust investment banking pipelines and described the markets environment as broadly risk-on, highlighted by an 86% year-on-year surge in equities revenue.
  • Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO, stated that perpetual operating leverage is unrealistic, noting that competitive dynamics will transfer AI efficiency gains to clients.
  • Jeremy Barnum, CFO, disclosed that JPMorgan passed on select data center transactions due to weak credit underwriting structures.

Key metrics

  • Net income $16.9 billion
  • Diluted EPS $6.14
  • ROTCE 23%
  • Revenue ex-significant items growth 15% year-on-year
  • CIB revenue $24.9 billion up 27% year-on-year
  • Equities revenue growth 86% year-on-year
  • Standardized CET1 ratio 14.1% down 20 basis points versus prior quarter
  • AUM $5.1 trillion up 18% year-on-year

Financial highlights

  • Net income reached $16.9 billion with a ROTCE of 23% and total expenses of $27.3 billion, up 15% year-on-year.
  • Corporate and Investment Bank revenue rose 27% year-on-year to $24.9 billion, supported by 30% growth in investment banking fees and an 86% increase in equities revenue.
  • Consumer and Community Banking net income was $5.3 billion on revenue of $20.3 billion, up 8% year-on-year, adding over 500,000 net new checking accounts.
  • Asset and Wealth Management revenue grew 19% year-on-year to $6.9 billion, generating $50 billion in long-term net inflows and bringing AUM to $5.1 trillion.
  • Total credit costs were $2.5 billion, comprising $2.4 billion in net charge-offs and $149 million in net reserve builds.

Guidance

  • Jeremy Barnum, CFO, raised full-year 2026 NII ex-Markets guidance from $95 billion to about $96.5 billion.
  • Jeremy Barnum, CFO, expects full-year 2026 total NII of approximately $105.5 billion and Markets NII of about $9 billion.
  • Jeremy Barnum, CFO, raised full-year 2026 adjusted expense guidance from $105 billion to about $107.5 billion, with $1.5 billion driven by capital markets outperformance.
  • Jeremy Barnum, CFO, expects the full-year 2026 card net charge-off rate to be approximately 3.2%.
  • Jeremy Barnum, CFO, declared a third-quarter 2026 dividend of $1.65 per share.

Q&A

  • Ken Usdin, Autonomous Research: What is the rationale for elevating Doug and Troy to co-presidents and what does it imply for CEO tenure, and how sustainable is the strength in investment banking and markets?
    Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO: Dimon stated Marianne Lake chose to retire upon learning of plans to name co-presidents and confirmed the CEO succession timetable remains unchanged. Barnum noted investment banking pipelines remain robust, while equities strength was exceptionally elevated reflecting a broad risk-on environment.
    answered directly
  • John McDonald, Truist Securities: What drove the upward revision to NII ex-Markets guidance, and why is markets NII guidance higher despite higher rate expectations?
    Jeremy Barnum, Chief Financial Officer: Barnum attributed the NII ex-Markets increase to about $96.5 billion to higher wholesale and consumer deposits alongside higher rates, while Markets NII was raised to $9 billion due to lower non-interest-bearing financing assets and a $150 million capital allocation shift to CIB.
    answered directly
  • James Mitchell, Seaport Global Securities: Could AI slow expense growth to create operating leverage over the intermediate term, and is there any update on regulatory capital proposal adjustments?
    Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO: Dimon rejected targeting perpetual operating leverage, stating AI efficiency benefits ultimately accrue to customers through competition, while advocating for the elimination of double counts in operating and market risk capital.
    answered directly
  • Mike Mayo, Wells Fargo: How do you view operating leverage given positive dollar growth, what drove the $2.5 billion expense guidance raise, and why is Troy qualified to lead consumer banking?
    Jeremy Barnum, Chief Financial Officer: Barnum explained that $1.5 billion of the $2.5 billion expense increase was already incurred due to $6.5 billion in capital markets revenue outperformance, with $1 billion added for the second half, while Dimon cited Troy's operational expertise and analytical breadth.
    answered directly
  • Gerard Cassidy, RBC: Are competitors showing credit underwriting excesses, and could a stable regulatory environment emerge to support bank valuations?
    Jeremy Barnum, Chief Financial Officer: Barnum noted JPMorgan passed on select data center financing deals due to weak underwriting, while Dimon cited mild covenant loosening, with both expressing optimism regarding regulatory clarity.
    answered directly

Notable quotes

  • “It's getting close to as good as it gets. We just don't know how long it's going to last.” Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO
  • “The notion that somehow you can forever increase your operating leverage is a crazy notion.” Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO
  • “The market is clearly extremely risk on, and we're kind of takers of that.” Jeremy Barnum, Chief Financial Officer
  • “The ultimate beneficiary of AI will be our customers.” Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO

What changed

  • Guidance: NII ex-Markets raised from about $95 billion to about $96.5 billion for full-year 2026.
  • Guidance: Total NII raised from approximately $103 billion to approximately $105.5 billion for full-year 2026.
  • Guidance: Markets NII raised from about $8 billion to about $9 billion for full-year 2026.
  • Guidance: Adjusted expenses raised from about $105 billion to about $107.5 billion for full-year 2026.
  • Guidance: Card net charge-off rate revised from approximately 3.4% to approximately 3.2% for full-year 2026.
  • New mentions: Doug and Troy co-presidents, Sapphire Preferred card refresh, Chase UK, Smart cash tool, Token expense, Data center underwriting, Frontier Labs
  • Dropped: Basel III Endgame and G-SIB reproposals, Private credit growth and systemic risk exposure, Cybersecurity risks and LLM-enabled threats, Kinexys and stablecoins
  • Tone: Management shifted focus from regulatory mitigation and private credit concerns toward capital markets revenue outperformance and management succession.

Risks mentioned

  • Mild loosening in credit covenants and expense add-backs across competitive markets.
  • Potential pressure on deposit costs if interest rates remain elevated for an extended period.
  • Uncertain duration of the current elevated risk-on market conditions and capital markets outperformance.
  • Weak credit underwriting structures in specialized growth areas like data center financing.

Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite4.5

JPMorgan Chase reported fiscal Q2 2026 net income of $16.9 billion, or $6.14 per share, with a ROTCE of 23%. Revenue ex-significant items grew 15% year-on-year, driven by strong equities and investment banking performance.

Key takeaways

  • Jamie Dimon stated the board appointed two co-presidents to prepare them for expanded responsibilities, noting this has not altered the timetable for the CEO role and prompted Marianne Lake's retirement.
  • Jeremy Barnum reported that wholesale deposit growth outperformed expectations due to lending environment tailwinds, while consumer deposit growth expects low single-digit growth for the year.
  • Jamie Dimon noted artificial intelligence is being deployed across nearly 1,000 use cases to drive efficiency, though ultimate benefits will accrue to customers in a competitive market.
  • Jeremy Barnum noted token expenses are forecast to accelerate meaningfully in the second half, though the full-year contribution remains trivial.

Key metrics

  • Net Income $16.9 billion
  • EPS $6.14
  • ROTCE 23%
  • Standardized CET1 Ratio 14.1% down 20 basis points versus prior quarter
  • CCB Revenue $20.3 billion up 8% year-on-year
  • CIB Revenue $24.9 billion up 27% year-on-year
  • AUM $5.1 trillion up 18% year-on-year
  • Client Assets $7.7 trillion up 19% year-on-year

Financial highlights

  • Net income was $16.9 billion on revenue growth of 15% year-on-year (ex-significant items).
  • Expenses were $27.3 billion, up 15% year-on-year.
  • Credit costs totaled $2.5 billion, consisting of $2.4 billion in net charge-offs and a $149 million net reserve build.
  • Consumer and Community Banking (CCB) revenue was $20.3 billion, up 8% year-on-year, with net income of $5.3 billion.
  • Corporate and Investment Bank (CIB) revenue was $24.9 billion, up 27% year-on-year, with net income of $9.7 billion.

Guidance

  • Full Year 2026 NII ex-Markets: revised up to $96.5 billion from $95 billion
  • Full Year 2026 Total NII: $105.5 billion
  • Full Year 2026 Markets NII: about $9 billion
  • Full Year 2026 Adjusted Expense: $107.5 billion
  • Full Year 2026 Card Net Charge-Off Rate: approximately 3.2%

Q&A

  • Ken Usdin, Autonomous Research: Can you evaluate the recent management changes and elevation of Doug and Troy to co-presidents, and what does it mean for your tenure as CEO?
    Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO: The board appointed two co-presidents to prepare them for broader roles without changing the CEO timetable, and Marianne Lake decided to retire upon learning of the plans.
    answered directly
  • John McDonald, Truist Securities: What are the drivers of the upward revision to NII ex-Markets and its cadence, and what drives higher markets NII despite higher rates?
    Jeremy Barnum, Chief Financial Officer: Full-year NII ex-Markets was raised from $95 billion to $96.5 billion due to wholesale and consumer deposit balances and slightly higher rates, while Markets NII is expected up from lower non-interest-bearing assets and a $150 million equity shift.
    answered directly
  • James Mitchell, Seaport Global Securities: Is there a time over the intermediate term where expense growth could slow and operating leverage becomes more likely? Any update on regulatory proposals like G-SIB surcharge and Basel III?
    Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO: Operating leverage growth could slow in 2027 or 2028, and regulatory changes should fix calculations, remove double counts in operating and market risk capital, and adjust G-SIB and wholesale funding rules.
    answered directly
  • Ebrahim Poonawala, Bank of America: What is the health of the Main Street consumer and commercial lending? Also, why buy back stock at three times tangible book instead of keeping excess capital for a rainy day?
    Jeremy Barnum, Chief Financial Officer: Consumer spend remains robust, delinquencies are lower than expected, and capital will be deployed organically at a 17% return while remaining open to M&A.
    answered directly
  • Glenn Schorr, Evercore: What is the status and rollout timeline for the smart cash tool, and what are the European consumer banking aspirations?
    Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO: The smart cash tool is a test case involving narrow customer segments with results expected this year, while digital expansion in the U.K. reached 2.5 to 3 million customers and Berlin opened with better-than-expected progress.
    answered directly

Notable quotes

  • “That's it. No mystery.” Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO
  • “It's getting close to as good as it gets. We just don't know how long it's going to last.” Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO
  • “Well, thank you. I'm glad to hear you're reading. My parents will be proud.” Jeremy Barnum, Chief Financial Officer

What changed

  • Guidance: NII ex-Markets full year 2026: $95 billion moved to $96.5 billion
  • Guidance: Total NII full year 2026: $103 billion moved to $105.5 billion
  • Guidance: Markets NII full year 2026: $8 billion moved to about $9 billion
  • Guidance: Adjusted Expense full year 2026: $105 billion moved to $107.5 billion
  • Guidance: Card Net Charge-Off Rate full year 2026: 3.4% moved to approximately 3.2%
  • New mentions: Sapphire Preferred card, Chase UK, Berlin digital bank, Smart cash tool, Apple business, CHIPS Act, Fiserv, FIS, Stripe, PayPal, Cash App, Block, Chime, SoFi, Revolut
  • Dropped: Private credit and leveraged lending, NBFI exposure
  • Tone: The tone shifted toward discussing management succession following the appointment of co-presidents, alongside observations that the current environment is nearing peak cycle performance.

Risks mentioned

  • Mild deterioration in revenue assumptions, add-backs, PIK, and covenants among some competitors in data center underwriting.

Gemini 3.1 Pro4.5

JPMorgan Chase reported fiscal Q2 2026 net income of $16.9 billion and EPS of $6.14, driven by a 15% year-on-year increase in revenue excluding significant items. Equities revenue surged 86% year-on-year, though Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO, noted this level of performance is statistically improbable to repeat exactly.

Key takeaways

  • Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO, stated that the timetable for his tenure remains essentially the same at several years, though the board recently elevated Doug and Troy to co-presidents to prepare them for more responsibilities.
  • Jeremy Barnum, Chief Financial Officer, expects consumer deposit growth to be low single-digit this year, while wholesale deposit growth was quite strong in the first half.
  • Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO, noted the firm has about 50 important artificial intelligence use cases across risk, fraud, and marketing, expecting huge efficiencies.
  • Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO, stated the firm is testing a smart cash tool that will be available this year for a narrow segment of accounts.

Key metrics

  • Net income $16.9 billion
  • EPS $6.14
  • Revenue (excluding significant items) up 15% year-on-year
  • Expenses $27.3 billion up 15% year-on-year
  • Equities revenue up 86% year-on-year
  • IB fees up 30% year-on-year
  • Standardized CET1 ratio 14.1% down 20 basis points versus the prior quarter
  • Net new checking account growth over 500,000 accounts this quarter

Financial highlights

  • Net income reached $16.9 billion, with an EPS of $6.14 and a ROTCE of 23%.
  • Revenue excluding significant items rose 15% year-on-year, while expenses increased 15% year-on-year to $27.3 billion.
  • Equities revenue increased 86% year-on-year, and investment banking fees were up 30% year-on-year.
  • Credit costs totaled $2.5 billion, including $2.4 billion in net charge-offs and a $149 million net reserve build.

Guidance

  • Jeremy Barnum, Chief Financial Officer, expects full year 2026 NII ex-Markets of about $96.5 billion.
  • Jeremy Barnum, Chief Financial Officer, anticipates full year 2026 total NII of approximately $105.5 billion.
  • Jeremy Barnum, Chief Financial Officer, projects full year 2026 Markets NII of about $9 billion.
  • Jeremy Barnum, Chief Financial Officer, expects full year 2026 adjusted expense of about $107.5 billion.
  • Jeremy Barnum, Chief Financial Officer, anticipates a full year 2026 card net charge-off rate of approximately 3.2%.

Q&A

  • Ken Usdin, Autonomous Research: Can you evaluate the recent management changes elevating Doug and Troy to co-presidents and what it means for your tenure? Also, how do you judge the sustainability of the strength in investment banking and markets?
    Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO: The board made them co-presidents to prepare them for more responsibilities, and Marianne decided to retire; the timetable for my tenure hasn't changed. Investment banking saw some pull forward from large deals, but the pipeline remains robust. Equities had an exceptionally strong quarter that is hard to imagine repeating, though the overall environment feels good.
    answered directly
  • John McDonald, Truist Securities: What are the drivers of the upward revision to NII ex-Markets and the cadence heading into next year? Also, what is driving the higher guidance for markets NII despite higher rates?
    Jeremy Barnum, Chief Financial Officer: The upward revision to NII ex-Markets is primarily driven by higher deposit balances across wholesale and consumer, as well as slightly higher rates. Markets NII is guided higher due to expected lower amounts of financed non-interest-bearing assets in the second half, and a small shift of equity allocation into the CIB.
    answered directly
  • James Mitchell, Seaport Global Securities: Is there a time over the intermediate term where expense growth could slow and operating leverage becomes more likely? Also, is there any update on potential adjustments to regulatory proposals like the G-SIB surcharge and Basel III?
    Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO: The notion of forever increasing operating leverage is crazy, though we might see a slowdown in expense growth around 2027 or 2028. On regulation, they should do the numbers the right way, remove double counts in operating and market risk capital, and adjust the G-SIB and short-term wholesale funding rules fairly.
    answered directly
  • Ebrahim Poonawala, Bank of America: What are you seeing regarding consumer fragility and CapEx broadening beyond AI? Also, why buy back stock at three times tangible book instead of saving capital for a rainy day or M&A?
    Jeremy Barnum, Chief Financial Officer: Consumer spend remains robust and delinquencies are lower than expected, supported by a resilient labor market. We prefer to deploy capital organically at a 17% return or through M&A rather than buying back stock at high prices, as buybacks are an investment decision.
    answered directly
  • Gerard Cassidy, RBC: Are you seeing excesses in credit underwriting from competitors? Also, could we enter a period of regulatory stability that enhances bank valuations?
    Jeremy Barnum, Chief Financial Officer: We have passed on some data center deals due to underwriting standards, noting some mild deterioration in covenants and rollover risk in the market. Regulatory stability is desirable and we hope regulators focus on true safety and soundness rather than adding bureaucratic reporting.
    answered directly

Notable quotes

  • “The notion that somehow you can forever increase your operating leverage is a crazy notion.” Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO
  • “It's getting close to as good as it gets. We just don't know how long it's going to last.” Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO
  • “The goal is to deploy our capital at a 17% return. That is the goal.” Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO
  • “We are going to use AI to do a better job for our clients. That's our job.” Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO

What changed

  • Guidance: Full year 2026 NII ex-Markets increased from about $95 billion to about $96.5 billion.
  • Guidance: Full year 2026 total NII increased from approximately $103 billion to approximately $105.5 billion.
  • Guidance: Full year 2026 Markets NII increased from about $8 billion to about $9 billion.
  • Guidance: Full year 2026 adjusted expense increased from about $105 billion to about $107.5 billion.
  • Guidance: Full year 2026 card net charge-off rate decreased from approximately 3.4% to approximately 3.2%.
  • New mentions: Doug and Troy, Marianne, Sapphire Preferred card refresh, Smart cash tool, Chase UK, Frontier Labs, Token expense
  • Dropped: Private Credit, Cyber Risk
  • Tone: Management maintained a cautious tone regarding the macroeconomic environment, though Jamie Dimon explicitly noted that current conditions are getting close to as good as it gets.

Risks mentioned

  • Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO, warned that the firm does not know exactly when the negative convexity of deposit rates will kick in.
  • Jeremy Barnum, Chief Financial Officer, noted mild deterioration in covenants and rollover risk in the market, leading the firm to pass on some data center deals.
  • Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO, cautioned that the exceptionally strong quarter in equities was driven by a combination of factors that is statistically improbable to repeat exactly.

Q&A

  • Ken Usdin, Autonomous Research: Can you evaluate recent management changes and elevation of Doug and Troy to co-presidents, and what this means for leadership development and your tenure as CEO?
    Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO: Marianne is exceptional individual. Board made decision to create two co-presidents to prepare them for more at company. Hasn't changed timetable. Marianne decided she'd rather retire than stay, result of knowing about succession plans. No mystery.
    direct
  • Ken Usdin, Autonomous Research: How do you judge sustainability of investment banking and markets strength, and how risk-on are the various businesses?
    Jeremy Barnum, CFO: Investment banking fees historically fine but not super peak, had room to come up. Some pull forward happened, large deals contributed meaningfully. Pipeline quite robust; high-profile activity seems encouraging more activity. Markets elevated for time; equities exceptionally strong this quarter with highly dynamic conditions. Separation between fixed income up 6% and equities up 86% year-over-year.
    direct
  • Chris McGratty, KBW: Can you discuss progression toward 15% retail market share target and how higher-for-longer rates impact pace of market share gains?
    Jeremy Barnum, CFO: Wholesale deposit growth strong this quarter and first half, outperforming expectations due to franchise strength, winning deals, and lending environment particularly BFI space and data center stuff. Consumer expecting low single-digit growth, unchanged expectation, balance between robust net new checking accounts and yield-seeking flows. View that 15% will be outcome of executing strategy across components from primary bank relationships, branch expansion, deepening product-value proposition.

  • Chris McGratty, KBW: Where are we in the investment cycle for branch build-out, AI investments, and banker hires, and how does this play into operating leverage outlook?
    Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO: Complete continuation of what we've been doing for years. Shouldn't expect any change. Power of franchise is such that able to aggressively invest for future generating returns and solidifying competitive position while delivering exceptional current returns. Would be true delivering 15%, 16%, 17% returns. When delivering these types, firing on all cylinders.
    direct
  • John McDonald, Truist Securities: Can you talk about drivers of upward revision to NII ex-Markets from $95 billion to $96.5 billion and cadence in Q3, Q4, and exit rate?
    Jeremy Barnum, CFO: Biggest single factor is deposit balances across wholesale and consumer, overall quantum and mix shift in favor of slightly higher margin. Rates higher than previously guided in short and back end. Sensitivity a little more than EAR suggests due to consumer betas outperformance, disproportionately in front end. Increase as function of higher rates but most is balances overall.
    direct
  • John McDonald, Truist Securities: Markets NII guided higher even though rate outlook higher. What are drivers, balance sheet mix?
    Jeremy Barnum, CFO: Markets NII actually liability sensitive. Higher rates would expect it down; instead forecast up. Difference is changes in balance sheet composition, expecting lower finance non-interest-bearing assets in second half. One balance sheet unit drives number quite a bit, can overwhelm rate effect. Also increased equity allocation to CIB this quarter moves some equity out of corporate into CIB; markets NII moving out of NII ex-Markets into markets NII, about $150 million not expected to offset on bottom line.
    direct

Subscription subagent · 1079 words · 5 verified quotes

Pharma: pipeline readouts, guidance raise
LLY · fiscal Q2 2026 call, reported 2026-08-05 · 119 transcript paragraphs

Claude Fable 5.19.0

Lilly's Q2 revenue grew 48% year over year, with MOUNJARO and ZEPBOUND contributing $14.9B (+$6.3B) and non-GAAP EPS of $8.38 including $3.03 of acquired IPR&D charges, versus $6.31 a year ago. Lucas Montarce, CFO, raised full-year revenue guidance to $85B-$87B from $82B-$85B and the performance margin range to 49%-50.5%. Dave Ricks, Chair and CEO, said that while Lilly is pleased with progress, "we're not satisfied".

Key takeaways

  • Ilya Yuffa, President of Lilly USA, said U.S. Foundayo volume almost doubled in the last week of July versus a month earlier, with about one in four new starts and prescribers up from about 8,000 to 36,000.
  • Dave Ricks, Chair and CEO, said retatrutide's BLA classification is in active litigation with the FDA, with submission expected by Q1 2027 once more CMC data are gathered.
  • The Medicare GLP-1 Bridge Program launched July 1 for 20 million eligible Americans at $50 a month; Yuffa said around 80% of early patients are on injectables and 60%-70% are new to treatment.
  • Lucas Montarce, CFO, said ZEPBOUND net price will go down "for sure" as CVS access resumes in Q4, embedded in the guide and more than offset by volume.

Key metrics

  • Revenue growth +48% vs Q2 2025
  • MOUNJARO + ZEPBOUND revenue $14.9B +$6.3B YoY
  • Non-GAAP EPS $8.38 vs $6.31; incl. $3.03 IPR&D
  • Non-GAAP performance margin 54.8% Up 9 points YoY
  • Gross margin 86.3% Up ~1.3 points YoY
  • U.S. price -3% -9% ex rebate estimate changes
  • U.S. obesity incretin TRx +78% ~6 of 10 are Lilly

Financial highlights

  • Revenue up 48%; U.S. revenue up 33% on volume, with U.S. price down 3% (down 9% excluding rebate estimate changes).
  • Constant-currency growth of 55% in Europe (including a $250M Jardiance milestone), 30% in Japan, 93% in China and 136% in rest of world.
  • Gross margin 86.3%, up about 1.3 points; marketing, selling and administrative up 25%; R&D up 14% with more than 40 phase III programs.
  • Non-GAAP performance margin 54.8%, up nine points; Ebglyss sales more than doubled and Jaypirca grew 56%.

Guidance

  • Lucas Montarce, CFO, raised full-year revenue to $85B-$87B from $82B-$85B, a $2.5B midpoint increase, with second-half growth significant in dollars but slower in percentage terms.
  • Montarce raised the non-GAAP performance margin to 49%-50.5% from 47%-48.5% and set non-GAAP EPS at $35.50-$36.50, up $2.78 at the midpoint before the $3.03 Q2 acquired IPR&D charge.
  • Montarce said prior-period rebate adjustments will not recur in the second half, which also carries Europe Q3 and U.S. Type 2 diabetes Q4 seasonality.
  • Dave Ricks, Chair and CEO, expects Foundayo U.S. Type 2 diabetes action later this year; Patrik Jonsson, President of Lilly International, expects the major OUS rollout at the beginning of 2027; Dan Skovronsky, Chief Scientific and Product Officer, expects the retatrutide submission in Q1 2027.

Q&A

  • Asad Haider, Goldman Sachs: Why has the U.S. Foundayo launch been slower than anticipated, when will it inflect, and how is the ex-U.S. race against Novo's oral shaping up?
    Ilya Yuffa, President of Lilly USA and Global Customer Capabilities: CVS added Foundayo in June, Bridge launched July 1 and full DTC began; in the last week of July volume almost doubled with about one in four new starts on Foundayo. Patrik Jonsson, President of Lilly International: the UAE launch started strongly and Lilly held incretin share despite a second oral; Mexico and Saudi Arabia are next, most launches in 2027.
    Answered partially; called Novo's share claim noisy sell-in data.
  • Courtney Breen, Bernstein: What milestones remain for retatrutide under the BLA pathway, and will the classification apply to later incretins?
    Dave Ricks, Chair and CEO: Lilly holds retatrutide is a biologic under the amino acid count rule; the first court decision was mixed and litigation is active. All clinical data are in hand, more CMC data must be gathered, and submission is expected by Q1 next year.
    Answered partially; later incretins not addressed.
  • Akash Tewari, Jefferies: Guidance implies a second-half deceleration despite Bridge and Foundayo access; is price being missed or is the team conservative?
    Lucas Montarce, Chief Financial Officer: The guide rose $2.5B; recent quarters included one-off rebate adjustments that will not continue, the 2025 base had a bolus of new-country MOUNJARO launches that will not repeat, and Europe Q3 and U.S. Type 2 diabetes Q4 seasonality are factored in; second-half dollar growth is still significant.
    Answered directly; disputed the premise.
  • Umer Raffat, Evercore: ZEPBOUND net price looks like about $580 per prescription, well above cash prices; how sustainable is the medical-exception delta now CVS is back?
    Lucas Montarce, Chief Financial Officer: LillyDirect runs $299 to $449, Bridge $245, plus the commercial price; medical exceptions and larger commercial script sizes lift the per-prescription figure. CVS access resumes in Q4, medical exceptions are "maybe a short-term thing", and price is expected to go down for sure, embedded in the guide.
    Answered partially; no net price figure.

Unanswered

  • Terence Flynn, Morgan Stanley: Is Foundayo sampling dampening weekly IQVIA prescription data? Ilya Yuffa, President of Lilly USA, described the sampling program and prescriber count but did not say whether it dampens the data.
  • Jason Gerberry, Bank of America: Are psychiatry and vaccines scalable priorities or one-off deals? Jake Van Naarden, President of Lilly Oncology and Head of Business Development, would not forward-look, saying Lilly will be opportunistic.
  • Courtney Breen, Bernstein: Will BLA classification apply to subsequent incretins? Dave Ricks, Chair and CEO, addressed only retatrutide.

Notable quotes

  • “While we're pleased with the progress this year so far, we're not satisfied.” Dave Ricks, Chair and CEO
  • “We're starting to see an inflection point on Foundayo now.” Ilya Yuffa, President of Lilly USA and Global Customer Capabilities
  • “I think we are extremely well-positioned here in terms of supply. There will be no gating of launches either.” Patrik Jonsson, President of Lilly International
  • “The medical exception is maybe a short-term thing and our all along view is to have open access for all the patients.” Lucas Montarce, Chief Financial Officer

What changed

  • Guidance: Full-year revenue: $82B-$85B to $85B-$87B.
  • Guidance: Non-GAAP performance margin: 47%-48.5% to 49%-50.5%.
  • Guidance: Non-GAAP EPS: $35.50-$37 to $35.50-$36.50, midpoint up $2.78 before the $3.03 Q2 acquired IPR&D charge.
  • Guidance: Retatrutide: TRIUMPH-1 topline due Q2 to U.S. submission in Q1 2027 under a disputed BLA pathway.
  • Guidance: ZEPBOUND net price: relatively stable to expected to decline from Q4.
  • New mentions: Onswik (insulin efsitora alfa), Curevo, LimmaTech Biologics and The Vaccine Company, AtaiBeckley (BPL-003), VERVE-102 PCSK9 gene editing; tersolisib; brenipatide, UAE, Mexico and Saudi Arabia Foundayo launches; Novo Nordisk oral competition
  • Dropped: Lilly Employer Connect and employer coverage, Incretin pricing-versus-volume economics and the MFN agreement, Obesity market segmentation and eloralintide, Part D 2028 plans
  • Tone: Q1 closed with "a lot more to come in 2026" and high confidence that Foundayo was tracking to plan; Q2 conceded a slower U.S. launch while asserting an inflection, Dave Ricks closed with "we're not satisfied", and retatrutide language turned to litigation with the FDA.

Risks mentioned

  • Foundayo's U.S. launch curve was slower than anticipated, and the inflection claim rests on a few weeks of data.
  • ZEPBOUND net price is expected to fall from Q4 as CVS access resumes and medical exceptions fade, per Montarce.
  • Retatrutide's BLA pathway is disputed with the FDA, with a mixed first court decision, per Ricks.
  • Novo Nordisk's oral is scaling in the U.K., Germany and the UAE and generic semaglutide has launched in India and Brazil, per Jonsson.
