Claude Sonnet 5.58.5

Lilly's fiscal Q2 2026 revenue grew 48% versus Q2 2025, with MOUNJARO plus ZEPBOUND at $14.9B, up $6.3B, and non-GAAP EPS of $8.38 including $3.03 of acquired IPR&D charges. Lucas Montarce, CFO, raised full-year revenue guidance to $85B-$87B, a $2.5B increase at the midpoint, and non-GAAP performance margin to 49%-50.5%. US price fell 3%, and the US Foundayo launch drew questions on pace.

Key takeaways

  • Ilya Yuffa, President, Lilly USA and Global Customer Capabilities, said Foundayo volume in the last week of July almost doubled versus a month earlier, with about one in four new starts on Foundayo, and US prescribers rose from 8,000 to 36,000.
  • Ilya Yuffa said the Medicare GLP-1 Bridge launched July 1, with about 80% of people treated on injectables and 60%-70% new to treatment.
  • Dave Ricks, Chair and CEO, said Lilly expects to submit retatrutide by Q1 next year under a BLA pathway, after gathering a little more CMC data.
  • Montarce said US price is expected to decline as medical exceptions fade with CVS access from Q4, with volume growth more than offsetting it, and this is embedded in the guide.
  • Patrik Jonsson, President, Lilly International, said Lilly's international incretin share is about 55% in a market up 74%, and Foundayo's major ex-US launches come at the beginning of 2027.

Key metrics

  • Revenue growth 48% vs Q2 2025
  • MOUNJARO plus ZEPBOUND revenue $14.9B $6.3B of growth vs Q2 2025
  • Non-GAAP EPS $8.38 $6.31 in Q2 2025; includes $3.03 acquired IPR&D
  • Non-GAAP performance margin 54.8% up nine points
  • US revenue up 33% price down 3%
  • US obesity incretin TRx up 78% Lilly about 6 in 10 TRx
  • Foundayo US prescribers 36,000 8,000 at last call
  • INLURIYO US new Rx share over 50% metastatic breast cancer oral SERD market

Financial highlights

  • Gross margin was 86.3% of revenue, up about 1.3 points, and non-GAAP performance margin was 54.8%, up nine points.
  • Marketing, selling and administrative expenses rose 25% and R&D rose 14%, with more than 40 active phase III programs.
  • US revenue rose 33% with price down 3%, or down 9% excluding rebate and discount estimate changes; Europe rose 55%, China 93% and rest of world 136% in constant currency.
  • Oncology, immunology and neuroscience medicines grew 121%, with Jaypirca up 56% and Ebglyss sales more than doubling.
  • Lilly returned $1.5B in dividends and $1.6B in share repurchases.

Guidance

  • Montarce guided FY2026 revenue to $85B-$87B, with the low end raised by $3B and the high end by $2B.
  • Montarce guided non-GAAP performance margin to 49%-50.5%, up 2 points at both ends, and non-GAAP EPS to $35.50-$36.50, after the $3.03 Q2 acquired IPR&D charge.
  • Ricks expects US regulatory action for Foundayo in Type 2 diabetes later this year, with approvals in additional markets later this year and a global rollout in all major markets in 2027.
  • Dan Skovronsky, Chief Scientific and Product Officer, expects the retatrutide BLA submission in Q1 2027, EU approval of insulin efsitora alfa in the coming months, and lebrikizumab every-12-weeks data in 2027.
  • Montarce expects H2 growth to be more driven by OUS penetration, because Q2 included one-off prior-period US rebate and discount adjustments and the 2025 base had a bolus of new-country MOUNJARO launches.

Q&A

  • Asad Haider, Goldman Sachs: Why has the US Foundayo launch curve been slower than anticipated, and how does it compete with Novo's oral pill outside the US, including in the UAE?
    Ilya Yuffa, President, Lilly USA and Global Customer Capabilities: Yuffa said CVS added Foundayo in June, Bridge launched July 1, and in the last week of July volume almost doubled versus a month earlier, with about one in four new starts on Foundayo. Patrik Jonsson, President, Lilly International, said Lilly's combined incretin share held steady in the UAE with a second oral entering, next launches are Mexico and Saudi, most launches come in 2027, and Novo's figure is noisy sell-in data including inventory build.
    Answered directly, pushing back on the Novo comparison.
  • Courtney Breen, Bernstein: What are the requirements and milestones for retatrutide to be accepted as a BLA, and would the pathway apply to later incretins?
    Dave Ricks, Chair and CEO: Ricks said Lilly believes retatrutide is a biologic under the amino acid count rule, the first court decision was mixed and litigation is active, and Lilly has the clinical data but must gather a little more on CMC. He expects submission by Q1 next year.
    Answered partially: gave the timing but not the read-through to later incretins.
  • Akash Tewari, Jefferies: Does guidance imply a back-half revenue deceleration despite Bridge and Foundayo ramping, and is the guide conservative?
    Lucas Montarce, Chief Financial Officer: Montarce said Q2 included one-off prior-period US rebate and discount adjustments not expected to continue in H2, and the 2025 base had a bolus of new-country MOUNJARO launches. He said Q3 Europe and Q4 US Type 2 diabetes seasonality are factored in, and dollar growth is still expected to be significant.
    Answered partially: explained phasing but did not say whether the guide is conservative.
  • Umer Raffat, Evercore: Net price per obesity prescription looks well above cash benchmarks; how sustainable is that, and does it drive favourable rebate adjustments?
    Lucas Montarce, Chief Financial Officer: Montarce said channel prices are LillyDirect $299 to $449, Bridge $245, plus commercial. He said CVS access from Q4 removes the need for medical exceptions, which he called short term, and he expects price to go down, offset by more volume.
    Answered partially: channel prices given but no net price figure.
  • Terence Flynn, Morgan Stanley: How extensive is the Foundayo sampling program, is it dampening IQVIA prescription data, and how much has the prescriber base expanded?
    Ilya Yuffa, President, Lilly USA and Global Customer Capabilities: Yuffa said sampling covers the first 30 days of the first dose, extensively across prescribers, with full supply, and prescribers rose from 8,000 at the last call to 36,000.
    Answered partially: gave prescriber numbers but did not address IQVIA data.
  • Dave Risinger, Leerink: UAE Foundayo sales of $31M were close to half the $67M in the US; what is the ex-US obesity potential?
    Patrik Jonsson, President, Lilly International: Jonsson said it is very early days with an initial inventory build, most patients are incretin-naive, and the major OUS rollout comes at the beginning of 2027. He said Lilly is probably slightly better positioned on Type 2 reimbursement given three head-to-head trials, but OUS reimbursement takes time.
    Answered partially: no ex-US potential figure.

Unanswered

  • Umer Raffat, Evercore: What is the net price per obesity prescription, and does it drive favourable rebate adjustments each quarter? Lucas Montarce, CFO, listed channel prices and said price will go down, but gave no net price figure.
  • Akash Tewari, Jefferies: Is the guidance conservative given the implied H2 deceleration? Montarce explained the H2 phasing and the $2.5B upgrade without saying whether the guide is conservative.
  • Terence Flynn, Morgan Stanley: Is Foundayo sampling dampening IQVIA prescription data? Ilya Yuffa, President, Lilly USA and Global Customer Capabilities, described the sampling program and did not address the IQVIA effect.
  • Geoff Meacham, Citibank: Does Lilly need a commercial strategy to protect against retatrutide that is apparently available today? Ken Custer, President, Lilly Cardiometabolic Health, said people await authentic retatrutide and will vote with their feet, and described no specific strategy.

Notable quotes

  • “While we're pleased with the progress this year so far, we're not satisfied.” Dave Ricks, Chair and CEO
  • “We have a pretty large lead over any competitors with a triple-acting medicine.” Dave Ricks, Chair and CEO
  • “it's very early days, and there is a lot of noise in the data.” Patrik Jonsson, President, Lilly International
  • “The medical exception is maybe a short-term thing and our all along view is to have open access for all the patients.” Lucas Montarce, Chief Financial Officer
  • “people will vote with their feet and embrace the really guaranteed product.” Ken Custer, President, Lilly Cardiometabolic Health

What changed

  • Guidance: FY2026 revenue guidance moved from $82B-$85B to $85B-$87B.
  • Guidance: Non-GAAP performance margin guidance moved from 47%-48.5% to 49%-50.5%.
  • Guidance: Non-GAAP EPS guidance moved from $35.50-$37 to $35.50-$36.50, after the $3.03 Q2 acquired IPR&D charge.
  • Guidance: Retatrutide timing moved from TRIUMPH-1 top-line results later in Q2 to a BLA submission in Q1 2027.
  • New mentions: TRIUMPH program, Onswik (insulin efsitora alfa), Curevo, LimmaTech Biologics, The Vaccine Company, AtaiBeckley (BPL-003), VERVE-102, tersolisib, brenipatide
  • Dropped: Employer coverage and Employer Connect, Medicare Bridge 2028 outlook, Obesity market segmentation (retatrutide, eloralintide)
  • Tone: Last quarter Foundayo was described as tracking to expectations, with Lucas Montarce, CFO, saying 'We feel very confident on the trajectory that we've seen so far'; this quarter executives defend a slower US launch and Dave Ricks, Chair and CEO, says 'we're not satisfied'.

Risks mentioned

  • Montarce said US price is expected to decline as medical exceptions fade, with volume growth needed to offset it.
  • Q2 included one-off prior-period US rebate and discount adjustments that Montarce does not expect to continue in H2.
  • Jonsson said generic semaglutide launched in India and Brazil, and Canada tirzepatide TRx grow only at the pace of the total market.
  • Ricks said the first court decision on retatrutide's biologic status was mixed and litigation is active.
  • Custer indicated unauthorised retatrutide supply is apparently available today.

Non-GAAP EPS grew to $8.38 despite absorbing $3.03 per share in acquired IPR&D charges, while non-GAAP gross margin expanded 130 basis points to 86.3%. Full-year 2026 revenue guidance was raised to $85 billion to $87 billion, reflecting ongoing volume demand across international incretin markets and expanding U.S. channel access.

  • Combined global sales of Mounjaro and Zepbound reached $14.9 billion, an increase of $6.3 billion compared to Q2 2025.
  • Non-GAAP performance margin expanded 9 percentage points year-over-year to 54.8%, prompting an upward revision in full-year margin guidance to 49% to 50.5%.
  • U.S. Foundayo prescriber adoption expanded from 8,000 at the prior earnings call to 36,000, with volume nearly doubling in the final week of July versus June.
  • Clinical data packages for retatrutide are complete, with an anticipated BLA submission targeted for Q1 2027 pending additional CMC package finalization.
  • Capital allocation included $1.5 billion distributed in dividends and $1.6 billion deployed in share repurchases during the quarter.

  • Non-GAAP gross margin reached 86.3%, an increase of 1.3 percentage points compared to Q2 2025.
  • Operating expenses expanded with marketing, selling, and administrative expenses rising 25% and R&D spending increasing 14% year-over-year.
  • International markets posted double-digit to triple-digit constant currency revenue growth, led by Rest of World up 136%, China up 93%, Europe up 55%, and Japan up 30%.

Restaurant: comps, traffic, unit growth
Chipotle Mexican Grill, Inc. · fiscal Q2 2026 call, reported 2026-07-29 · 121 transcript paragraphs

Claude Sonnet 5.59.0

Chipotle's fiscal Q2 2026 revenue rose 9.3% to $3.3B, with comparable restaurant sales up 2.2% including a 1% transaction comp, versus 0.5% in Q1. Restaurant level margin was 25.2%, down 220bp, and adjusted diluted EPS was $0.33, flat y/y. Adam Rymer, CFO, guided full-year comps to the low single digits and Q3 comps to around plus 1%, assuming a roughly 200bp Cyclospora-related softness persists.

Key takeaways

  • Adam Rymer, CFO, said traffic improved through Q2 and into early July, then softened by about 200bp in the second half of July around the industry Cyclospora issue.
  • Scott Boatwright, CEO, said Chipotle is not involved in the Cyclospora conversation and is affected on sales only, since the implicated products are not on the menu.
  • Boatwright said HEAP gives two to four hours of labor efficiency, will be in 2,000 restaurants by year-end, and should cover the whole portfolio sometime in 2027.
  • Boatwright said the affordability scores were better in Q2 than in probably the past couple of years, and the younger and lower-income cohorts improved the most, per Rymer.
  • Boatwright said a frictionless in-restaurant rewards pilot begins in August, since only about 20% of in-restaurant transactions scan versus nearly 90% of digital.

Key metrics

  • Revenue $3.3B, up 9.3% vs Q2 2025
  • Comparable restaurant sales up 2.2% transaction comp of 1%
  • Restaurant level margin 25.2% down 220bp y/y
  • Adjusted diluted EPS $0.33 flat y/y
  • Digital sales $1.3B, 38.3% of sales 35.5% in prior year
  • New restaurants opened 101 including 80 Chipotlanes
  • Rewards members 23 million active about 20% of in-restaurant transactions scan
  • HEAP installs more than 1,000 restaurants 2 to 3 more entrees in peak 15 minutes

Financial highlights

  • Revenue was $3.3B, up 9.3%, with comparable restaurant sales up 2.2% and pricing impact of about 1.6%.
  • Restaurant level margin was 25.2%, down 220bp y/y, with cost of sales at 29.7% (up about 80bp), labor at 25.0% (up about 30bp) and other operating costs at 14.9% (up about 90bp).
  • Digital sales were $1.3B, or 38.3% of sales versus 35.5% a year ago.
  • Adjusted diluted EPS was $0.33, flat y/y, with non-GAAP G&A of $176M.
  • Chipotle repurchased $631M of stock at an average $32.55, ended with $800M of cash, restricted cash and investments and no debt, and had $1.7B of authorization remaining after an additional $1.3B.

Guidance

  • Rymer guided full-year comparable restaurant sales growth to the low single-digit range, versus about flat last quarter, and Q3 comps to around plus 1% assuming the roughly 200bp Cyclospora impact persists.
  • Rymer guided Q3 menu pricing to the mid 2% range and full-year pricing to the high end of 1%-2%, with price matching inflation from Q4.
  • Rymer guided Q3 cost of sales to just under 30%, labor to the mid 25% range, other operating costs to the mid 15% range and marketing to the low 3% range.
  • Rymer guided Q3 non-GAAP G&A to around $180M, full-year depreciation to around 3% of sales and the underlying tax rate to 24%-26%.
  • Boatwright expects about 350 new company-owned openings this year, about 80% with a Chipotlane, and national launch of catering and Build-Your-Own Chipotle in 2027.

Q&A

  • Dennis Geiger, UBS: More color on the low-single-digit comp guide, including Q3 and the recent softening.
    Adam Rymer, Chief Financial Officer: Rymer said Q2 traffic improved through the quarter and into the first half of July, then softened by about 200bp in the second half of July. He said Q3 is the toughest lap and comps are expected around plus 1% if the impact continues. Scott Boatwright, CEO, said Chipotle is not involved in the Cyclospora conversation.
    Answered directly.
  • David Palmer, Evercore ISI: Where does confidence in momentum into 2027 come from, given tough comparisons, a recent slowdown and 1%-2% comps?
    Scott Boatwright, Chief Executive Officer: Boatwright said Q2 transaction growth was early days of Recipe for Growth, with proof points in guest satisfaction, food quality and operational consistency. He said menu innovation, rewards engagement, brand communication and group occasions will build through H2 2026 and into 2027.
    Answered partially: no quantified evidence for 2027.
  • Danilo Gargiulo, Bernstein: Has the incremental capex and sales uplift from the restaurant remodel or refresh program been sized?
    Scott Boatwright, Chief Executive Officer: Boatwright said Chipotle has never done an aesthetic remodel and is stage-gating a refresh at several locations at roughly $100,000-$300,000 per restaurant. He said he has nothing to report yet.
    Answered partially: gave the cost range but no results.
  • Jon Tower, Citi: With a new CMO, how will the brand be positioned given For Real has gone stale, and will value be highlighted more?
    Scott Boatwright, Chief Executive Officer: Boatwright said the new work is the next evolution of For Real, with more on fresh preparation and ingredients, and value framed as extraordinary value from best ingredients, not price points or discounts. First elements are visible later this quarter.
    Answered partially: pushed back on the stale premise and gave no specifics.
  • Brian Mullan, Piper Sandler: What could be better in promoting loyalty in restaurant, and do non-loyalty transactions over-index to lower-income guests?
    Scott Boatwright, Chief Executive Officer: Boatwright said the non-loyalty 20% of in-store transactions are broad-based, not lower-income, and could see meaningful lift if enrolled. He said an August one-stop pay pilot will award points on payment and allow enrollment without relying on the restaurant team.
    Answered directly.
  • Peter Saleh, BTIG: Are the new menu items center-of-the-plate or sides, and any more detail?
    Scott Boatwright, Chief Executive Officer: Boatwright said the culinary team is working on center-of-the-plate items, beverage, sides and desserts. He said some big ideas could be on the calendar in the back half of 2026 but certainly in 2027.
    Answered partially: no specific items named.

Unanswered

  • David Palmer, Evercore ISI: What gives confidence in 2027 momentum given tough comparisons and a recent slowdown? Scott Boatwright, CEO, cited early proof points and green shoots without quantified evidence.
  • Danilo Gargiulo, Bernstein: Has the capex and sales uplift from the refresh program been sized? Boatwright gave a cost of $100,000-$300,000 per restaurant and said 'I have nothing to report today'.
  • Jon Tower, Citi: Will brand positioning highlight value and ingredient quality more now that For Real has gone stale? Boatwright said it is not a 180 pivot and described value only in general terms.
  • Peter Saleh, BTIG: Which new menu items are coming in the months ahead? Boatwright named categories such as center-of-the-plate, sides and beverage but no specific items.

Notable quotes

  • “We're not impacted, or we are impacted from a sales perspective, but we're not involved in the Cyclospora conversation today.” Scott Boatwright, Chief Executive Officer
  • “Very difficult to predict how long this could last.” Adam Rymer, Chief Financial Officer
  • “I have nothing to report today, unfortunately, but I'll tell you that work is in flight.” Scott Boatwright, Chief Executive Officer
  • “I do think there's a point of diminishing returns. I don't know if that's the fifth or sixth time around.” Scott Boatwright, Chief Executive Officer
  • “I say we're early days, the momentum is already building, and we have more levers that we will continue to pull as 2026 unfolds.” Scott Boatwright, Chief Executive Officer

What changed

  • Guidance: Full-year comparable restaurant sales moved from about flat to the low single-digit range.
  • Guidance: Full-year menu pricing moved from 1%-2% to near the high end of 1%-2%.
  • Guidance: Cost of sales guide moved from about 30% of sales (Q2) to just under 30% (Q3).
  • Guidance: Labor moved from the low 25% range to the mid 25% range, and other operating costs from the high 14% range to the mid 15% range.
  • Guidance: Marketing moved from below 3% of sales to the low 3% range, and non-GAAP G&A from around $181M to around $180M.
  • Guidance: Full HEAP portfolio coverage moved from late 2027 or early 2028 to sometime in 2027.
  • New mentions: Linebacker, Expo, Cook to Needs tool, Summer of Extras, Matchday BOGO, Monterrey (Alsea), Seoul (SPC Group), Saudi Arabia
  • Dropped: Hospitality and labor investment, Marketing spend returns, Delivery premium and the Zipline drone pilot, Happier Hour value test
  • Tone: Last quarter Adam Rymer, CFO, said 'We really just wanna remain cautious on our outlook'; this quarter the guide moved up to low single digits, but the discussion is dominated by a roughly 200bp late-July softness he calls 'Very difficult to predict how long this could last'.

Risks mentioned

  • Rymer said Q3 is the toughest comparison lap and a roughly 200bp late-July softness tied to the industry Cyclospora issue is assumed to continue.
  • Restaurant level margin fell 220bp y/y to 25.2%, with cost of sales up about 80bp and other operating costs up about 90bp.
  • Rymer said price of about 1% in H1 trailed inflation in the low to mid 3% range, and Q3 pricing in the mid 2% range trails inflation near 3%.
  • Boatwright said repeating LTOs reaches diminishing returns, perhaps the fifth or sixth time.
  • Boatwright said the in-restaurant loyalty enrollment process is 'clunky' and only about 20% of in-restaurant transactions scan.

Financial highlights

  • Revenue $3.3B, up 9.3%; comps +2.2% with transactions +1% and pricing about 1.6%; digital sales $1.3B, or 38.3% of sales versus 35.5%.
  • Restaurant-level margin 25.2%, down 220 bps: cost of sales 29.7% (+80 bps on beef and freight), labor 25% (+30 bps), other operating costs 14.9% (+90 bps), marketing 3%.
  • Adjusted diluted EPS $0.33, flat; non-GAAP G&A $176M; 101 openings including 80 Chipotlanes.
  • $631M repurchased at an average $32.55 (over $1.3B year to date), with $1.7B of authorization remaining; $800M of cash and no debt.

Guidance

  • Adam Rymer, CFO, raised full-year comps to low single digits from about flat and guided Q3 comps to around +1%, assuming the roughly 200 bps Cyclospora-related softening continues against the toughest lap of the year.
  • Rymer expects Q3 pricing in the mid 2% range (full year near the high end of 1%-2%), cost of sales just under 30%, labor in the mid 25% range, other operating costs in the mid 15% range and marketing in the low 3% range.
  • Rymer guided Q3 non-GAAP G&A to around $180M, depreciation around 3% of sales and a 24%-26% tax rate, with pricing and inflation expected to match from Q4.
  • Scott Boatwright, CEO, expects approximately 350 openings in 2026 (about 80% Chipotlanes), HEAP in about 2,000 restaurants by year-end, a frictionless rewards pilot in August and a national catering launch in 2027.

Q&A

  • Dennis Geiger, UBS: What is behind the low single-digit comp guide, the recent softening and the cadence from here?
    Adam Rymer, Chief Financial Officer: Traffic improved through Q2 and the first half of July, then comps softened about 200 bps around the industry Cyclospora issue; Q3 is guided to around +1% assuming that continues. Scott Boatwright, CEO, said the implicated products are not on Chipotle's menu and its lettuce is sourced in California.
    Answered directly.
  • Brian Vaccaro, Raymond James: Does the +1% Q3 guide with a 200 bps headwind imply about +3% underlying, and where is Max 15 throughput without HEAP?
    Adam Rymer, Chief Financial Officer: Correct; the guide assumes the impact persists through the quarter. HEAP restaurants do two or three more entrees in Max 15; system in-store Max 15 is in the low 20s, digital adds mid-teens, for a combined low-to-mid 30s per restaurant per day.
    Answered directly.
  • Chris Carril (on for Chris O'Cull), KeyBanc: How does the margin outlook beyond Q3 look as the pricing-inflation gap narrows?
    Adam Rymer, Chief Financial Officer: The gap was widest in the first half, with pricing around 1% and inflation in the low-to-mid 3% range; in Q3 inflation should be about 3% versus pricing in the mid 2% range, and from Q4 pricing and inflation are expected to match.
    Answered directly.
  • Danilo Gargiulo, Bernstein: Has Chipotle sized remodel capex and any sales uplift, and will it use partners in other international markets?
    Scott Boatwright, Chief Executive Officer: Chipotle has never done an aesthetic remodel; it is stage-gating a test at $100,000-$300,000 per restaurant across several geographies with nothing to report yet. Alshaya, Alsea and SPC operate as partners or JVs, while Chipotle owns Western Europe.
    Answered partially; no uplift or timeline.

Unanswered

  • Jim Salera, Stephens: What is the LTO contribution to traffic uplift versus historical benchmarks? Adam Rymer, CFO, said no specific LTO lifts are disclosed, offering only that LTOs typically deliver hundreds of basis points.
  • Danilo Gargiulo, Bernstein: Is there an early read on remodel sales uplift and timeline? Scott Boatwright, CEO: "I have nothing to report today, unfortunately, but I'll tell you that work is in flight."
  • David Palmer, Evercore ISI: What gives confidence that momentum picks up into 2027 given heavy spending for 1%-2% comps? Boatwright cited green shoots; the digital and brand plans were called industry changing but not shared.

Notable quotes

  • “Very difficult to predict how long this could last.” Adam Rymer, Chief Financial Officer
  • “I say we're early days, the momentum is already building, and we have more levers that we will continue to pull as 2026 unfolds.” Scott Boatwright, Chief Executive Officer
  • “I just want to make sure we put that to rest.” Scott Boatwright, Chief Executive Officer
  • “I like the 350 number. The team likes it. We feel comfortable with it.” Scott Boatwright, Chief Executive Officer

What changed

  • Guidance: Full-year comps: about flat to low single digits.
  • Guidance: Pricing: about 1.5% in Q2 to mid 2% in Q3; full year from 1%-2% to near the high end.
  • Guidance: Cost of sales: about 30% to just under 30%; labor: low 25% to mid 25%; other operating: high 14% to mid 15%; marketing: below 3% to low 3%.
  • Guidance: HEAP full portfolio: late 2027 or early 2028 to 2027; catering national launch: before end of 2026 to 2027.

  • Dropped: Zipline drone delivery pilot, Happier Hour pricing test, Delivery premium and third-party aggregators, Check mix and group-size normalization
  • Tone: Q1 was cautious ("remain cautious on that outlook"); Q2 was more assertive about momentum and wallet share gains, while Adam Rymer introduced an external headwind whose duration he called very difficult to predict.

Risks mentioned

  • Q3 is the toughest comparison of the year, per Rymer.
  • Beef and freight inflation and sequentially higher avocado prices, with first-half pricing of about 1% against inflation in the low-to-mid 3% range.
  • Investor pushback, raised by David Palmer of Evercore ISI, that heavy spending is yielding only 1%-2% comps.

    Scott Boatwright, Chief Executive Officer: Chipotle Honey Chicken performed better the second time around, though there may be a point of diminishing returns after several reintroductions. Our brand tracker showed solid progress across all income groups and age cohorts on value perception, with affordability scores better in Q2 than in the past couple of years. Value is also about convenience, execution, and menu innovation, and we are capitalizing on the emerging protein trend.
    answered directly

We remain confident in building momentum for the rest of the year, which supports our low single-digit full-year guidance.
    answered directly
  • Brian Vaccaro, Raymond James: Is it right to interpret the 1% Q3 comp guide as an underlying 3% trend minus a 2% Cyclospora headwind, and where is Max 15-minute throughput for stores without HEAP?

  • Brian Mullan, Piper Sandler: Can you elaborate on the efforts to promote the loyalty program in-restaurant, and do the 80% of in-store transactions without loyalty over-index to lower-income guests?

We are working to make the in-restaurant loyalty experience more frictionless, as the current process of using the app and paying separately is clunky. In August, we will introduce a one-stop pay system where guests automatically earn points when they pay, which will improve execution without impacting throughput.
    answered directly
  • Chris Carril, KeyBanc: Can you provide an update on how you are thinking about the margin outlook beyond the current quarter and the gap between pricing and inflation?
    Adam Rymer, Chief Financial Officer: The gap between price and inflation is easing in the second half of the year as we continue our measured rolling pricing strategy. In Q3, inflation will be closer to 3% while pricing will be in the mid 2% range, narrowing the dislocation seen in the first half. From Q4 forward, we expect pricing and inflation to match, eliminating that dislocation.
    answered directly

Chipotle reported second-quarter revenue of $3.3 billion, up 9.3% year-over-year, alongside comparable restaurant sales growth of 2.2%. Management raised full-year comparable restaurant sales guidance to the low single-digit range, citing ongoing benefits from the Recipe for Growth strategy and successful menu innovation despite a temporary mid-July softening caused by industry Cyclospora issues.

Key takeaways

  • Comparable restaurant sales increased 2.2% in the second quarter, supported by a transaction comp of 1%.
  • A mid-July softening of about 200 basis points occurred due to industry Cyclospora issues, leading to a Q3 comp guide of around plus 1%.
  • Digital sales reached $1.3 billion, representing 38.3% of total sales.
  • The High-Efficiency Equipment Package (HEAP) is driving two to four hours of daily in-restaurant efficiency and improving throughput.

Key metrics

  • Revenue $3.3 billion up 9.3% year-over-year
  • Comparable restaurant sales 2.2% includes transaction comp of 1%
  • Digital sales $1.3 billion 38.3% of total sales compared to 35.5% in prior year
  • Restaurant level margin 25.2% down 220 basis points year-over-year
  • Adjusted diluted EPS $0.33 flat to last year
  • New restaurants opened 101 new restaurants includes 80 Chipotlanes and 1 international partner-operated restaurant
  • Cost of sales 29.7% increase of about 80 basis points from last year
  • Share repurchases $631 million in second quarter at an average price of $32.55

Financial highlights

  • Revenue grew 9.3% year-over-year to $3.3 billion.
  • Restaurant level margin was 25.2%, down 220 basis points year-over-year.
  • Adjusted diluted EPS was flat year-over-year at $0.33.
  • Cost of sales was 29.7%, an increase of about 80 basis points from last year.
  • Labor costs were 25%, up about 30 basis points year-over-year.

Guidance

  • Full-year comparable restaurant sales growth: low single-digit range
  • Q3 pricing impact: mid 2% range
  • Full-year pricing impact: near the high end of the 1%-2% range
  • New restaurant openings: approximately 350 restaurants for full year 2026
  • HEAP installation target: approximately 2,000 restaurants by year-end 2026
  • Q3 comparable sales: around a plus 1%

Q&A

  • Dennis Geiger, UBS: Can you provide color on the comp guide for 2026, Q3 trajectory, and recent softening trends?
    Adam Rymer, Chief Financial Officer: Traffic improved through Q2 and early July, but the second half of July saw a 200 bps softening due to Cyclospora industry issues; Q3 comps anticipate around plus 1%, and full year guidance is raised to low single-digit range.
    answered directly
  • Brian Vaccaro, Raymond James: Does the 1% Q3 comp guide assume an underlying 3% trend with a sustaining 2% Cyclospora headwind, and what is overall Max 15 throughput?
    Adam Rymer, Chief Financial Officer: Adam Rymer confirmed the 1% guide assumes the 200 bps headwind persists through the quarter, and noted overall Max 15 throughput is low 20s in-store, mid-teens for digital, and low-to-mid 30s combined per restaurant daily.
    answered directly
  • Chris Carril, KeyBanc: What is the margin outlook beyond Q3 regarding the gap between pricing and inflation?
    Adam Rymer, Chief Financial Officer: In Q3, inflation is expected closer to 3% while pricing is mid-2%, and from Q4 forward, pricing and inflation are expected to match, eliminating the dislocation.
    answered directly
  • Drew North, Baird: Were there regional divergences around the World Cup, and what gave comfort to trend towards the higher end of pricing guidance?
    Adam Rymer, Chief Financial Officer: Restaurants within 10 miles of World Cup stadiums saw a small comp benefit of 50 to 100 bps with total quarter impact under 5 bps, and a methodical pricing rollout allowed precise resistance measurement giving comfort on the guidance.
    answered directly

Notable quotes

  • “Throughput is the cornerstone of great hospitality.” Scott Boatwright, Chief Executive Officer
  • “At Chipotle, our people are our greatest competitive advantage.” Scott Boatwright, Chief Executive Officer
  • “Opening a Chipotle nearly every day of the year is no small feat...” Adam Rymer, Chief Financial Officer
  • “Very difficult to predict how long this could last.” Adam Rymer, Chief Financial Officer

What changed

  • Guidance: Full-year comparable restaurant sales growth raised from 1%-2% to low single-digit range.
  • Guidance: HEAP installation target increased from more than 1,000 restaurants to approximately 2,000 restaurants by year-end 2026.
  • New mentions: Chipotle Honey Chicken, Cilantro Lime Sauce, High-Efficiency Equipment Package (HEAP), Summer of Extras, Matchday BOGO, Alsea, SPC Group, Alshaya, Arlie Sisson, Fernando Machado, Hamid Harizi
  • Dropped: Iran conflict impact, Happier Hour test
  • Tone: Management expressed increased confidence in full-year trends by raising guidance, while acknowledging a temporary industry-wide headwind from Cyclospora.

Risks mentioned

  • Cyclospora industry issues causing a softening of about 200 basis points in the second half of July.

Headline figures are right but guidance lacks attribution, the unanswered list is empty, risks are thin, and the what-changed section is largely wrong.

  • "Full-year comparable restaurant sales growth raised from 1%-2% to low single-digit range" - prior comp guidance was about flat; 1%-2% was the pricing range.
  • "HEAP installation target increased from more than 1,000 restaurants to approximately 2,000" - the year-end target was 2,000 on both calls; more than 1,000 is the current installed count.
  • New mentions list "Chipotle Honey Chicken", "Cilantro Lime Sauce", "High-Efficiency Equipment Package (HEAP)", "Summer of Extras", "Fernando Machado", "Arlie Sisson" - all were discussed on the Q1 call.
  • "A mid-July softening of about 200 basis points" - Rymer said the softening came in the second half of July.

Specialty insurer: combined ratio, small-cap
Kinsale Capital Group, Inc. · fiscal Q2 2026 call, reported 2026-07-24 · 71 transcript paragraphs

Claude Opus 5.59.0

Kinsale grew Q2 2026 diluted operating EPS 15.9% to $5.54 with a 75.5% combined ratio and 24.4% annualized operating ROE, even as gross written premium fell 5% on shrinkage in the Commercial Property Division. Excluding that division GWP rose 3.7%, and the company added a $250M buyback authorization while its pricing trend fell 5.9%.

Key takeaways

  • Michael Kehoe, Chairman, President and CEO, called commercial property "definitely a buyer's market" and said Kinsale is writing a shrinking volume there.
  • Stuart Winston, Chief Underwriting Officer, said London competition is spreading into long-tail casualty lines and depressing Construction pricing.
  • Kehoe raised buyback capacity to $337M and called it the principal capital allocation strategy while growth is limited.
  • Salmaan Allibhai, Chief Analytics and Technology Officer, put casualty loss cost trend at probably mid-single digits with little social inflation.

Key metrics

  • Diluted operating EPS $5.54 vs $4.78 in Q2 2025, +15.9%
  • Annualized operating ROE 24.4%
  • Gross written premium -5% YoY +3.7% ex Commercial Property Division
  • Combined ratio 75.5%
  • Expense ratio 21.7% vs 20.7% in Q2 2025
  • New business submissions +6% +8% ex Commercial Property Division
  • Pricing trend -5.9% vs -3.3% in Q1 2026

Financial highlights

  • Diluted operating EPS was $5.54, up 15.9% from $4.78, with net income up 31.1% and net operating earnings up 13.3%.
  • Gross written premium fell 5% and net written premium 1.4%, while net earned premium rose 8.9%.
  • The 75.5% combined ratio included 4.5 points of favorable prior-year development and 1.3 points of cat losses.
  • The expense ratio rose to 21.7% from 20.7% on a higher net commission ratio from larger reinsurance retentions.

Guidance

  • Bryan Petrucelli, CFO, said the Q2 expense ratio of 21.7% is a good measure going forward, with a possible slight commission uptick after the June 1 reinsurance renewal.
  • Michael Kehoe, Chairman, President and CEO, said Commercial Property comparisons ease modestly in H2 because 60% of the division's premium was written in last year's first half.
  • Stuart Winston, Chief Underwriting Officer, said nine new products launched this year, five more launch imminently and 10 are in the pipeline.
  • Petrucelli cited new money yields around 5.25% and average duration of four and a quarter years.

Q&A

  • Mark Hughes, Truist: When will property pricing reach equilibrium, should the accident year loss ratio drift up with mix, and how will buybacks be paced?
    Michael Kehoe, Chairman, President and CEO: Kehoe had no insight on timing and said Kinsale will keep leaning into buybacks with about $80M left on the prior $250M plus a new $250M; Salmaan Allibhai, Chief Analytics and Technology Officer, said the loss ratio could drift up over time.
    Answered partially; no timing or loss ratio figure.
  • Andrew Andersen, Jefferies: Is inbound account growth from broker engagement or pricing, and what are casualty loss trends?
    Stuart Winston, Chief Underwriting Officer: Winston said both, with an idle broker initiative re-engaging lapsed brokers and steady hit ratios; Salmaan Allibhai, Chief Analytics and Technology Officer, put casualty loss trend at mid-single digits, and Michael Kehoe said IBNR release is being slowed in longer-tail lines.
    Answered directly.
  • Pablo Singzon, JPMorgan: What drove the better current accident year loss ratio, and will the ceding commission keep rising?
    Salmaan Allibhai, Chief Analytics and Technology Officer: Allibhai cited normal variability with losses below expectations and reserves as conservative as ever; Bryan Petrucelli, CFO, said only one month of the June 1 treaty is in Q2, so a slight commission uptick may be offset by AI efficiency.
    Answered partially; no loss ratio driver isolated.
  • Hristian Getsov, Wells Fargo: How much are casualty ROEs compressing as E&S softens?
    Michael Kehoe, Chairman, President and CEO: Kehoe said everything is managed to a 20% ROE or greater, most lines run ahead of it, and quarterly ROE was 24.4%.
    Answered partially; broker commission question not addressed.

Unanswered

  • Hristian Getsov, Wells Fargo: Whether Kinsale will change broker commissions in a softer market. Michael Kehoe, Chairman, President and CEO, discussed service and risk appetite without indicating any change.
  • Dan Cohen, BMO: Whether Q2 is the trough for commercial property pricing. Stuart Winston, Chief Underwriting Officer, said there is "no telling where it's going to go in 2027."
  • Dan Cohen, BMO: The growth uplift from new products. Winston described a "crawl, walk, run" rollout with no quantification.

Notable quotes

  • “It's definitely a buyer's market, and as a consequence, we are writing a shrinking volume of business in that specific market.” Michael Kehoe, Chairman, President and CEO
  • “Kinsale shares represent a good value at today's price. A very good value.” Michael Kehoe, Chairman, President and CEO
  • “Growth driven by compromised profit margins is easy to manufacture but costly to unwind.” Stuart Winston, Chief Underwriting Officer
  • “It's hard to say. It could drift up over time.” Salmaan Allibhai, Chief Analytics and Technology Officer

What changed

  • Guidance: ROE target: low 20s or greater to 20% or greater.
  • Guidance: New money yield: around 5% to around 5.25%; duration slightly above four years to four and a quarter years.
  • Guidance: Buyback authorization: about $80M remaining to $337M after a new $250M authorization.
  • New mentions: Aspera (in-house retail broker, personal lines), Excess casualty, Commercial auto, Environmental, Energy, Inland Marine
  • Dropped: New granular disclosure of quotes and binds, Equity portfolio performance, Operating cash flow trend, Construction project delays
  • Tone: Kehoe moved from "We don't really have any good news to report there" on property to calling it "definitely a buyer's market," and leaned harder into buybacks, calling the shares "A very good value."

Risks mentioned

  • Michael Kehoe, Chairman, President and CEO, said the Commercial Property Division is shrinking amid competition from London MGAs and traditional markets.
  • Stuart Winston, Chief Underwriting Officer, said London competition is spreading into long-tail occurrence lines and general casualty.
  • Bryan Petrucelli, CFO, flagged a possible commission ratio uptick after the June 1 reinsurance renewal.
  • Salmaan Allibhai, Chief Analytics and Technology Officer, said the accident year loss ratio could drift up as mix shifts from property to casualty.

  • New mentions 'Excess casualty' and 'Commercial auto': both were cited by Winston on the Q1 call.

Claude Fable 5.19.0

Kinsale's Q2 diluted operating EPS rose 15.9% to $5.54 with a 24.4% annualized operating ROE and a 75.5% combined ratio, even as gross written premium fell 5% because the Commercial Property Division shrank in what Michael Kehoe, Chairman, President and CEO, called a buyer's market. Excluding that division, gross written premium grew 3.7%, and the board added $250M to the buyback authorization, bringing it to $337M.

Key takeaways

  • Michael Kehoe, Chairman, President and CEO, said the Commercial Property "crisis is over and rates are coming down pretty dramatically", so Kinsale is writing a shrinking volume there and prioritizing profitability over growth.
  • Combined pricing fell 5.9%, versus 3.3% in Q1, and Stuart Winston, Chief Underwriting Officer, said London competition in long-tail lines is creeping into other liability lines and depressing Construction pricing.
  • Salmaan Allibhai, Chief Analytics and Technology Officer, said losses came in below expectations and reserves are as conservative as ever, though the accident year loss ratio could drift up as mix shifts toward casualty.
  • Kehoe called Kinsale shares "a very good value" and said buybacks remain the principal capital allocation lever until growth accelerates.

Key metrics

  • Diluted operating EPS $5.54 vs $4.78, +15.9%
  • Operating ROE (annualized) 24.4% Q2 2026
  • Gross written premium -5% +3.7% ex Commercial Property
  • Combined ratio 75.5% 4.5 pts favorable PYD, 1.3 pts cat
  • Expense ratio 21.7% vs 20.7% in Q2 2025
  • Pricing trend -5.9% vs -3.3% in Q1
  • Buyback authorization $337M After new $250M

Financial highlights

  • Diluted operating EPS $5.54 versus $4.78; net income up 31.1%, net operating earnings up 13.3%; annualized operating ROE 24.4%.
  • Gross written premium down 5%, net written premium down 1.4%, net earned premium up 8.9%; ex Commercial Property, gross written premium up 3.7% (4.8% for the first half).
  • Combined ratio 75.5%, including 4.5 points of favorable prior year development (3.9 last year) and 1.3 points of cat losses; expense ratio 21.7% versus 20.7% on higher net commissions from higher reinsurance retentions.
  • Net investment income up 19.9%; float $3.4B versus $3.1B at year-end; new money yields around 5.25%; submissions up 6% (8% ex Commercial Property).

Guidance

  • Michael Kehoe, Chairman, President and CEO, expects the gross written premium comparison to get modestly easier in Q3 and Q4 because 60% of Commercial Property premium was written in the first half of last year.
  • Bryan Petrucelli, CFO, said the 21.7% Q2 expense ratio is a good measure going forward, with a slight uptick possible in net commissions from the June 1 reinsurance renewal, potentially offset by AI efficiency gains.
  • Stuart Winston, Chief Underwriting Officer, said five more product offerings launch imminently with 10 in the pipeline, after nine so far this year.
  • Kehoe said everything is managed to a 20% ROE or greater; Salmaan Allibhai, Chief Analytics and Technology Officer, put casualty loss cost trend at mid-single digits.

Q&A

  • Pablo Singzon, JPMorgan: What drove the accident year loss ratio improvement versus peers, and will the ceding commission rate keep rising after renewals?
    Salmaan Allibhai, Chief Analytics and Technology Officer: Losses came in below expectations as in recent quarters, with normal variability across a dozen statutory lines and reserves as conservative as ever. Bryan Petrucelli, CFO: the Q2 expense ratio is a good indication going forward, with a slight uptick possible since only one month of the June 1 treaty is reflected.
    Answered partially; no loss-pick detail.
  • Dan Cohen, BMO: Is Q2 the trough for commercial property, and what drives the 3.7% ex-property growth?
    Stuart Winston, Chief Underwriting Officer: There is no telling where property goes in 2027; London MGA pressure continues and Kinsale will keep pricing tactical and accept shrinking there. Long-tail competition from London is creeping into other liability lines and depressing Construction pricing; admitted-market flow exists but is not pervasive.
    Declined to call a trough.
  • Andrew Andersen, Jefferies: Is the rise in inbound accounts from broker engagement or pricing, and what is the view on social inflation and casualty loss trend?
    Stuart Winston, Chief Underwriting Officer: A bit of both: the idle broker initiative is increasing flow while hit ratios are roughly unchanged. Allibhai put casualty loss cost trend at mid-single digits with favorable losses across the board; Michael Kehoe, Chairman, President and CEO, said Kinsale has slowed IBNR releases in longer-tail occurrence business.
    Answered directly.
  • Mark Hughes, Truist: How did property pricing move Q2 versus Q1, should the accident year loss ratio drift up with mix, and how will buybacks be paced?
    Michael Kehoe, Chairman, President and CEO: Equilibrium will come because catastrophes happen, but there is no insight on timing; Winston said Agribusiness and small business property are more stable and pricing is nearing a floor. Allibhai said the loss ratio could drift up but current bookings look good. Buybacks continue: roughly $80M remained on the prior $250M plus the new $250M.
    Answered partially; no Q2-versus-Q1 pricing detail.

Unanswered

  • Hristian Getsov, Wells Fargo: How much are E&S casualty ROEs compressing as the market softens? Michael Kehoe, Chairman, President and CEO, restated the 20% ROE target and the 24.4% quarter without quantifying compression.
  • Mark Hughes, Truist: What granularity can be given on property pricing in Q2 versus Q1? Kehoe said there is no insight on when equilibrium comes; Stuart Winston, Chief Underwriting Officer, gave qualitative color only.
  • Dan Cohen, BMO: What growth uplift will the new products deliver? Winston said growth will be slow at a "crawl, walk, run pace", with no figures.

Notable quotes

  • “Kinsale shares represent a good value at today's price. A very good value.” Michael Kehoe, Chairman, President and CEO
  • “Obviously, the crisis is over and rates are coming down pretty dramatically.” Michael Kehoe, Chairman, President and CEO
  • “Growth driven by compromised profit margins is easy to manufacture but costly to unwind.” Stuart Winston, Chief Underwriting Officer
  • “I would just reiterate that our reserves are as conservative as they've ever been in our company's history.” Salmaan Allibhai, Chief Analytics and Technology Officer

What changed

  • Guidance: Buyback authorization: roughly $80M remaining to $337M after an additional $250M.
  • Guidance: ROE target: "low 20s or greater" to "20% or greater".
  • Guidance: Expense ratio: 21.1% in Q1 to 21.7% in Q2, now the go-forward measure.
  • Guidance: Pricing trend: -3.3% to -5.9%, now benchmarked to the MS Amlin index rather than Amwins.
  • Guidance: Casualty loss cost trend: newly stated at mid-single digits.
  • New mentions: Aspera (in-house retail broker for personal lines), Idle broker initiative, London MGAs, Inland Marine, Environmental and Energy lines, AI-driven operating efficiency
  • Dropped: New quarterly premium disclosure by underwriting division, Equity portfolio performance, E&S Homeowners decline, Construction activity delays and cash from operations
  • Tone: Q1 had "no good news to report" on property but stressed "lots of ways to grow"; Q2 declared the property crisis over with rates falling dramatically, leaned harder on buybacks and reserve conservatism, and Stuart Winston warned against growth from compromised margins.

Risks mentioned

  • Commercial Property rate declines with no telling where pricing goes in 2027, per Winston, under pressure from London MGAs and large shared and layered deals.
  • London competition spreading into liability occurrence lines, Construction and general casualty, per Winston.
  • Broker commission tension in a soft market and some flow to the admitted market, which Winston called not pervasive.
  • The accident year loss ratio could drift up as mix shifts from property to casualty, per Allibhai.

  • Expense ratio was 21.7% versus 20.7% in Q2 2025, with the other underwriting expense ratio at 10.3% versus 10.6%.

  • Net written premium fell 1.4% while net earned premium rose 8.9%; new business submissions rose 6%, or 8% excluding Commercial Property.

Guidance

  • Kehoe said Commercial Property Division y/y comparisons should be modestly easier in Q3 and Q4, because 60% of its premium was written in the first half of last year.
  • Kehoe announced an additional $250 million buyback authorization, bringing the current authorization to $337 million.
  • Bryan Petrucelli, CFO, said a slight uptick in ceding commissions is possible after the June 1 treaty renewals, and the Q2 expense ratio should be a good measure going forward.
  • Allibhai said the current accident year loss ratio could drift up over time given the mix shift, although losses are currently below expectations.
  • Winston said nine new products or enhancements have rolled out this year, five more launch imminently and another 10 are in the pipeline.

Q&A

  • Pablo Singzon, JPMorgan: What drove the improved current accident year loss ratio, and will the reinsurance ceding commission rate keep rising?
    Salmaan Allibhai, Chief Analytics and Technology Officer: Allibhai said losses came in below expectations with nothing out of the ordinary, just normal variability and mix across a dozen statutory lines. Bryan Petrucelli, CFO, said treaties renewed June 1 so only one month of new-treaty effect is in Q2, a slight uptick in commissions is possible, and Q2 is a good indicator going forward.
    Answered partially: no specific driver of the improvement.
  • Hristian Getsov, Wells Fargo: With E&S casualty softening, how much are ROEs compressing, and how is Kinsale thinking about broker commissions?
    Michael Kehoe, Chairman, President and CEO: Kehoe said Kinsale manages everything to a 20% ROE or greater, most lines probably run ahead of that, and Q2 operating ROE was 24.4%. He gave no commission plan and called the wholesaler-retailer commission split a normal tension in a soft market.
    Answered partially: ROE answered, commissions not.
  • Dan Cohen, BMO: Is Q2 the trough in Commercial Property, and what is driving the 3.7% growth ex Commercial Property?
    Stuart Winston, Chief Underwriting Officer: Winston said there is no telling where it goes in 2027, pressure continues from London MGAs, and Kinsale will keep pricing tactical and accept shrinking in that division. He said London long-tail competition is creeping into other liability occurrence lines, and some flow to the admitted market is not pervasive.
    Answered partially: declined to call a trough.
  • Andrew Andersen, Jefferies: How much of the increase in inbound accounts is broker engagement versus pricing, and what is the view on casualty loss trends?
    Stuart Winston, Chief Underwriting Officer: Winston said it is a bit of both, with the idle broker initiative re-engaging brokers. Allibhai said casualty loss cost trend is probably mid-single digits, and Kehoe said Kinsale is slowing IBNR release in longer-tail occurrence business while letting it flow out faster in short-tail lines.
    Answered directly.
  • Mark Hughes, Truist: When might property pricing stabilize, could the accident year loss ratio drift up, and how will buyback pacing look?
    Michael Kehoe, Chairman, President and CEO: Kehoe said equilibrium will eventually come but he has no insight into when. Allibhai said the loss ratio could drift up over time; Kehoe said the first $100 million authorization is exhausted and the $250 million has about $80 million left, plus the new $250 million.
    Answered partially: no timing on property pricing.
  • Dan Cohen, BMO: How have new products contributed to growth over the past year, and what is the potential uplift?
    Stuart Winston, Chief Underwriting Officer: Winston said new products are driving submissions and new premium but growth will be slow, with a crawl, walk, run pace, and Kinsale will ramp up as the market turns.
    Answered partially: no quantification of past contribution or future uplift.

Unanswered

EPS of $5.54 $4.78 Q2 2025, +15.9%

Key takeaways

  • Technology and AI competitive advantage

Financial highlights

    Salmaan Allibhai, Chief Analytics and Technology Officer: Losses came in below expectations as they have for several quarters, reflecting normal variability and business mix. Loss ratio is composite of a dozen statutory lines. Reserves are as conservative as company history. On commissions: second quarter provides good indication of forward trajectory; slight uptick in commission rate expected going forward; AI activities may provide efficiency gains on operating side; second quarter expense ratio should be good measure going forward.
    direct
  • Hristian Getsov, Wells Fargo: Given E&S casualty softening and GWP up only 3% versus 4% in Q1, how much are ROEs compressing given your mid-to-high single digit loss trend views? How are you thinking about broker commissions going forward?

On commissions: brokers critical to success and seeking to maximize economics; tension between wholesalers and retail clients on commission split; soft market rate declines pressure all parties. Accident year loss ratio hard to predict; could drift up; losses coming below expectations; feel good about loss ratio bookings and very good about IBNR reserve conservatism as positive for future performance.

Cyclical industrial: segment guidance
DE · fiscal Q3 2026 call, reported 2026-08-20 · 119 transcript paragraphs

Claude Opus 5.59.5

Deere grew fiscal Q3 2026 net sales and revenues 5% to $12.608B and earned $1.379B, or $5.10 per share, as construction and forestry sales rose 18% and production and precision ag fell 6%. Deere raised FY2026 net income guidance to $4.75B-$5B, and Deanna Kovar, President of Worldwide Agriculture & Turf, called 2026 the ag cycle bottom with a measured 2027 recovery.

Key takeaways

  • North American planter and sprayer early order programs are up mid-single digits versus last year's completed program, with See & Spray factory adoption nearly doubling.
  • Brent Norwood, CFO, said net tariffs will be a 2027 headwind, with the run rate near $1B versus about $750M net this year.
  • Chris Seibert, Director of Investor Relations, said C&F orders cover four to five months versus a typical two to three, on infrastructure, data centers and rental fleets.
  • Deere lowered its South America ag industry outlook to down 15%-20% and Europe to about flat.

Key metrics

  • Net sales and revenues $12.608B +5% YoY
  • Net income attributable to Deere $1.379B; $5.10 per diluted share
  • PPA net sales $3.998B -6% YoY
  • C&F net sales $3.618B +18% YoY; price +8 points
  • Tariff refunds recognized $110M in Q3 $382M FY2026 to date
  • C&F order book Four to five months on hand vs typical two to three months

Financial highlights

  • Equipment operations net sales rose 6% to $10.999B with a 14.4% operating margin, including $110M of tariff refunds.
  • Production and precision ag sales fell 6% to $3.998B, with operating margin of 13.2%.
  • Small ag and turf sales rose 12% to $3.383B with an 18.4% operating margin.
  • Construction and forestry sales rose 18% to $3.618B on 8 points of price, with a 12.1% operating margin.

Guidance

  • Dan Pooley, Manager of Investor Communications, raised FY2026 net income to $4.75B-$5B and equipment operations cash flow to $5B-$5.5B.
  • Pooley guided PPA sales down about 10% (11%-12% margin), small ag and turf up about 15% (14.5%-15.5%) and C&F up about 20% (10.5%-11.5%).
  • Chris Seibert, Director of Investor Relations, cut FY2026 direct tariff expense to about $1.1B from $1.2B, assuming no further refunds.
  • Brent Norwood, CFO, expects Q4 PPA and C&F sales similar to Q3, with lower PPA and small ag margins on seasonal costs and no refund benefit.

Q&A

  • Steve Fisher, UBS: Is the net 2026 tariff impact a headwind, tailwind or neutral for 2027?
    Brent Norwood, Chief Financial Officer: Norwood said about $1.1B of direct tariffs less $382M of refunds leaves roughly $750M net this year, and next year's run rate is closer to $1B, a step up.
    Answered directly.
  • Tami Zakaria, JPMorgan: What lowered the tariff estimate to $1.1B, and how is the excavator launch going?
    Chris Seibert, Director of Investor Relations: Seibert said Section 232 import rates fell from about 25% to 15% from June 1, covering five of 12 months and implying a 2027 tailwind; Brent Norwood, CFO, said three Deere-designed excavator models are shipping.
    Answered directly.
  • Chad Dillard, Bernstein: Why does C&F price fall in Q4 after +8% in Q3, and how big could dealer rental become?
    Chris Seibert, Director of Investor Relations: Seibert said Q3 lapped about 5% negative price a year earlier and full-year C&F price is 3 points; Brent Norwood, CFO, said 30%-35% of earthmoving transactions start as rentals but gave no size target.
    Answered partially.
  • Jamie Cook, Truist Securities: How does 2027 production versus retail set up, and what pricing should be expected?
    Chris Seibert, Director of Investor Relations: Seibert said PPA and C&F are each underproducing retail by a couple of points this year; Deanna Kovar, President of Worldwide Agriculture & Turf, said pricing aims to cover inflation without a figure.
    Answered partially; no 2027 price figure.

Unanswered

  • Peter Kalemkerian, Baird: Whether the 2028 European CAP policy change pulls 2027 demand forward, and a 2027 Europe forecast. Chris Seibert, Director of Investor Relations, said it is "too early to tell" and gave no forecast.
  • Angel Castillo, Morgan Stanley: Margin direction for next year and the $2B-$3B lifecycle parts opportunity after the FTC right-to-repair settlement. Seibert gave no margin direction and Brent Norwood, CFO, did not comment on the $2B-$3B figure.

Notable quotes

  • “At the same time, the underlying fundamentals continue to support a measured recovery rather than a sharp rebound in 2027.” Deanna Kovar, President, Worldwide Agriculture & Turf (PPA, Americas and Australia)
  • “We have about four to five months of orders on hand, which is quite frankly, a little more than we would want to have.” Chris Seibert, Director of Investor Relations
  • “So there will be a bit of a step up in our tariff expense next year as we compare to this year.” Brent Norwood, Chief Financial Officer

What changed

  • Guidance: Net income: $4.5B-$5B to $4.75B-$5B; operating cash flow $4.5B-$5.5B to $5B-$5.5B.
  • Guidance: PPA: sales down 5%-10% to down about 10%; margin 11%-13% to 11%-12%.
  • Guidance: Margins: small ag and turf 13.5%-15% to 14.5%-15.5%; C&F 10%-12% to 10.5%-11.5%.
  • Guidance: Industry: South America down about 15% to down 15%-20%; Europe flat to up 5% to about flat; earthmoving up about 5% to up 5%-10%.
  • Guidance: Tariffs: direct expense about $1.2B to about $1.1B; net about $900M to about $750M.
  • New mentions: Operations Center PRO Service, SmartGrade, Job site safety solutions, FTC right-to-repair settlement, Brazil move Agriculture Financing program, European CAP budget policy change (2028)
  • Dropped: Iran conflict, U.S. biofuel policy (RVO, E15), Precision Essentials renewals, Starlink / JDLink Boost
  • Tone: Last quarter Norwood said Deere's "baseline view remains" that 2026 is the ag cycle bottom; this quarter Kovar held that view but qualified it as "a measured recovery rather than a sharp rebound," and tariffs moved from a refund tailwind to a 2027 headwind.

Risks mentioned

  • Brent Norwood, CFO, expects net tariff expense to step up to around $1B in 2027 from about $750M.
  • Chris Seibert, Director of Investor Relations, cited input and commodity volatility, Black Sea shipment disruptions and softness in the E.U. and Brazil.
  • Deanna Kovar, President of Worldwide Agriculture & Turf, said fertilizer price uncertainty has farmers looking for alternatives.

Claude Fable 5.19.5

Deere's fiscal Q3 net sales and revenues rose 5% to $12.608B, with a 14.4% equipment operations margin and net income of $1.379B, or $5.10 per diluted share, helped by $110M of incremental IEPA tariff refunds. Dan Pooley, Manager of Investor Communications, presented a raised fiscal 2026 net income guide of $4.75B-$5B, and Brent Norwood, CFO, reiterated that 2026 is the bottom of the ag cycle with a measured 2027 recovery.

Key takeaways

  • Brent Norwood, CFO, said net tariffs become a headwind in fiscal 2027: about $750M net this year ($1.1B direct less $382M refunds) versus a run rate of about $1B next year.
  • Deanna Kovar, President of Worldwide Agriculture & Turf, said model year 2027 planter and sprayer early orders are up mid-single digits, with See & Spray adoption nearly doubling to about one-third of North American sprayers on order.
  • Chris Seibert, Director of Investor Relations, said Construction & Forestry holds four to five months of orders, more than the two to three Deere typically wants, with PPA and C&F each underproducing retail by a couple of points.
  • Kovar said South America demand is softer, with the industry cut to down 15%-20%, while used model-year 2023 and 2024 high horsepower tractors are down nearly 40% from a year ago.

Key metrics

  • Net sales and revenues $12.608B +5% YoY
  • Net income $1.379B $5.10 per diluted share
  • Equipment operations margin 14.4% Above expectations
  • PPA sales $3.998B -6% YoY, 13.2% margin
  • SAT sales $3.383B +12% YoY, 18.4% margin
  • C&F sales $3.618B +18% YoY, 12.1% margin
  • MY2027 planter and sprayer early orders +mid-single digits vs last year's program

Financial highlights

  • Equipment net sales up 6% to $10.999B; net income $1.379B, EPS $5.10; equipment operations margin 14.4%.
  • Production & Precision Ag sales down 6% to $3.998B at a 13.2% margin; Small Ag & Turf sales up 12% to $3.383B at an 18.4% margin.
  • Construction & Forestry sales up 18% to $3.618B at a 12.1% margin, including 8 points of price lapping about 5% negative price a year ago.
  • Financial Services net income $219M; IEPA refunds $110M in Q3 and $382M year to date.

Guidance

  • Dan Pooley, Manager of Investor Communications, raised fiscal 2026 net income to $4.75B-$5B from $4.5B-$5B, equipment operations cash flow to $5B-$5.5B from $4.5B-$5.5B and Financial Services to $870M from $860M.
  • Pooley guided PPA sales down about 10% (from down 5%-10%) at an 11%-12% margin, SAT up about 15% at a 14.5%-15.5% margin (from 13.5%-15%), and C&F up about 20% at a 10.5%-11.5% margin.
  • Pooley cut South America industry sales to down 15%-20% and Europe to about flat, and raised U.S. and Canada construction to up 5%-10%.
  • Brent Norwood, CFO, expects Q4 PPA and C&F sales similar to Q3 with no refund benefit and seasonally higher R&D and SA&G, and a fiscal 2027 net tariff run rate around $1B.

Q&A

  • Steve Fisher, UBS: Is the roughly $800M net tariff impact this year a headwind, tailwind or neutral going into 2027?
    Brent Norwood, Chief Financial Officer: A headwind: about $1.1B of direct tariffs less $382M of refunds gives net exposure of approximately $750M this year, while the 2027 run rate is expected right around $1B.
    Answered directly.
  • Kristen Owen, Oppenheimer: Did something slip from Q4 into Q3, and was tractor inventory built in Q3 ahead of the industry?
    Chris Seibert, Director of Investor Relations: Not to read too much into Q3; some demand was pulled ahead to manage Q4 risk and nothing on inventory is concerning. Norwood said Q4 PPA and C&F sales should be similar to Q3 without the refund benefit and with seasonally high R&D and SA&G, so margins come in a little.
    Answered directly; disputed the inventory premise.
  • Tami Zakaria, JPMorgan: Is the tariff estimate cut to $1.1B from relief or refunds, and how is the excavator launch trending?
    Chris Seibert, Director of Investor Relations: Mainly Section 232 changes cutting the rate on imported goods from roughly 25% to 15% effective June 1, so only five of 12 months benefit this year with a further 2027 tailwind. Norwood: three Deere-designed excavator models are in market, with more over three to four years.
    Answered directly.
  • Chad Dillard, Bernstein: Why does C&F guidance imply only about 50 bps of Q4 price after +8% in Q3, and how large should dealer rental fleets get?
    Chris Seibert, Director of Investor Relations: The C&F price guide went 2.5% to 3% to 2.5% and back to 3%; Q3's 8% partly reflected about 5% negative price in Q3 2025. Norwood said 30%-35% of earthmoving transactions start as a rental and dealers want to expand fleets, with no target size.
    Answered partially; declined to size rental fleets.

Unanswered

  • Tim Thein, Raymond James: How should the 2027 mix impact be thought about versus the 2- to 3-point benefit in stronger years? Chris Seibert, Director of Investor Relations, said it is too early to tell, citing input volatility and E.U. and Brazil softness.

  • “At the same time, the underlying fundamentals continue to support a measured recovery rather than a sharp rebound in 2027.” Deanna Kovar, President, Worldwide Agriculture & Turf Division, Production & Precision Ag
  • “We have about four to five months of orders on hand, which is quite frankly, a little more than we would want to have.” Chris Seibert, Director of Investor Relations
  • “So there will be a bit of a step up in our tariff expense next year as we compare to this year.” Brent Norwood, Chief Financial Officer

Risks mentioned

  • Net income attributable to Deere was $1.379B, or $5.10 per diluted share, with $110M of incremental tariff refunds recognized in Q3 and $382M year to date.
  • Production & Precision Ag net sales fell 6% to $3.998B with a 13.2% operating margin, while Small Ag & Turf rose 12% to $3.383B at 18.4%.
  • Construction & Forestry net sales rose 18% to $3.618B at a 12.1% operating margin, with price up 8 points.
  • Financial Services net income was $219M.

Guidance

  • Pooley guided FY2026 net income to $4.75B-$5B and equipment operations operating cash flow to $5B-$5.5B, with Financial Services net income of $870M.
  • Pooley guided Production & Precision Ag net sales down approximately 10% with an 11%-12% margin, Small Ag & Turf up approximately 15% with a 14.5%-15.5% margin, and Construction & Forestry up approximately 20% with a 10.5%-11.5% margin.
  • Pooley guided U.S. and Canada large ag industry sales down 15%-20%, South America down 15%-20%, Europe approximately flat, and U.S. and Canada construction equipment up 5%-10%.
  • Norwood expects Q4 net sales for Production & Precision Ag and Construction & Forestry similar to Q3, with no refund benefit and margins for PPA and Small Ag & Turf a little lower on seasonally higher R&D and SA&G.
  • Seibert said direct tariff expense is approximately $1.1B excluding refunds, with no further refunds assumed in the outlook.

Q&A

  • Steve Fisher, UBS: With roughly $800M of net tariff impact this year, is net tariff impact a headwind, tailwind or neutral for 2027?
    Brent Norwood, Chief Financial Officer: Norwood said net tariffs will be a headwind next year: about $1.1B of direct tariffs less $382M of refunds is roughly $750M net this year, and the 2027 run rate is closer to around $1B. He said there will be a bit of a step up in tariff expense versus this year.
    Answered directly with figures.
  • Tim Thein, Raymond James: How could mix, including higher technology take rates, affect 2027 compared with the 2- to 3-point mix benefit discussed in prior years?
    Chris Seibert, Director of Investor Relations: Seibert said it is too early to tell, especially in South America where things can turn quickly, citing input and commodity volatility, Black Sea disruptions and softness in the E.U. and Brazil. He said early order program signals will drive mix depending on how they wrap up.
    Deflected: no mix point figure.
  • Jerry Revich, Wells Fargo: What regional variability is behind the early order program, and could mid-single-digit growth end up high single digits?
    Deanna Kovar, President, Worldwide Ag & Turf, PPA, Sales and Marketing, Americas and Australia: Kovar said the programs are still open with a couple of weeks left on sprayers and a little longer on planters, and the U.S. is trending slightly better than Canada. She said time will tell whether mid-single digits turns more positive, citing See & Spray factory orders doubling and 40% of planters taking the most advanced technologies.
    Answered partially: would not call the final range.
  • Chad Dillard, Bernstein: Why does C&F guidance imply roughly 50bp of positive price in Q4 versus 8% in Q3, and does Deere want to grow dealer rental?
    Chris Seibert, Director of Investor Relations: Seibert said Q3 price of 8% was partly easy comps, since Q3 2025 had about 5% negative price from incentives, and Q4 has nothing outsized. Norwood said 30%-35% of earthmoving transactions start as a rental and dealer-owned fleets could boost inventory fill next year.
    Answered partially: did not reconcile the 50bp implied Q4 figure.
  • Jamie Cook, Truist Securities: What are the pricing expectations for 2027 given the early order program is up mid-single digits?
    Deanna Kovar, President, Worldwide Ag & Turf, PPA, Sales and Marketing, Americas and Australia: Kovar said early order program pricing was rolled out several months ago and the focus is covering inflation into 2027. Seibert said PPA and C&F are each modestly underproducing in 2026 by a couple of percentage points.
    Answered partially: no 2027 price percentage.
  • Peter Kalemkerian, Baird (for Mig Dobre): Could the 2028 EU CAP policy change pull demand forward into 2027, and is there a directional forecast for Europe in 2027?
    Chris Seibert, Director of Investor Relations: Seibert said it is too early to tell, order books run four to five months out and Q1 orders are just starting. He gave no pull-forward view and no 2027 forecast.
    Declined to forecast.

Unanswered

  • Tim Thein, Raymond James: How could mix and technology take rates affect 2027 versus the 2- to 3-point benefit of prior years? Chris Seibert, Director of Investor Relations, said it is too early to tell and gave no mix point figure.
  • Peter Kalemkerian, Baird (for Mig Dobre): Could the 2028 EU CAP change pull demand into 2027, and what is the 2027 Europe outlook? Seibert said it is too early to tell and gave no pull-forward view or forecast.
  • Jamie Cook, Truist Securities: What are the 2027 pricing expectations? Deanna Kovar, President, Worldwide Agriculture & Turf, said the focus is covering inflation and gave no percentage.
  • Angel Castillo, Morgan Stanley: What about merchandising incentives and the direction of margins into next year? Seibert said Deere is committed to cover inflation over time and offered no comment on incentives or directional margins.

Notable quotes

  • “As we discussed, the agricultural environment remains challenging, but we continue to believe that 2026 represents the bottom of the ag equipment cycle.” Brent Norwood, Chief Financial Officer
  • “At the same time, the underlying fundamentals continue to support a measured recovery rather than a sharp rebound in 2027.” Deanna Kovar, President, Worldwide Ag & Turf
  • “I think it is too early to tell, as you know, specifically in South America. Things can turn quickly.” Chris Seibert, Director of Investor Relations
  • “So there will be a bit of a step up in our tariff expense next year as we compare to this year.” Brent Norwood, Chief Financial Officer
  • “I would not read too much into the recent changes here in Q3.” Chris Seibert, Director of Investor Relations

What changed

  • Guidance: FY2026 net income guidance moved from $4.5B-$5B to $4.75B-$5B, and equipment operations cash flow from $4.5B-$5.5B to $5B-$5.5B.
  • Guidance: Financial Services net income moved from $860M to $870M, and direct tariff expense from $1.2B to $1.1B excluding refunds.
  • Guidance: Production & Precision Ag sales moved from down 5%-10% to down approximately 10%, and its margin from 11%-13% to 11%-12%.
  • Guidance: Small Ag & Turf margin moved from 13.5%-15% to 14.5%-15.5%, and Construction & Forestry margin from 10%-12% to 10.5%-11.5%.
  • Guidance: Europe industry sales moved from flat to up 5% to approximately flat, and South America from down about 15% to down 15%-20%.
  • Guidance: Global forestry moved from down 5% to down 10%, and U.S. and Canada construction equipment from up around 5% to up 5%-10%.
  • New mentions: Section 301 tariffs, SmartGrade, Job site safety solutions, Leap Ambitions, ExactEmerge, ExactRate, ExactShot, John Deere Operations Center
  • Dropped: Used and new inventory management, Segment diversification, Iran conflict
  • Tone: The prior call held the baseline that 2026 is the bottom with 'some level of recovery in 2027' and said it was very early on early orders; this call cites early orders up mid-single digits and frames 2027 as 'a measured recovery rather than a sharp rebound'.

Risks mentioned

  • Norwood said 2027 net tariffs will be a headwind, with a run rate closer to around $1B versus roughly $750M net this year.
  • Kovar said overall market conditions remain challenging, and U.S. and Canada large ag industry sales are guided down 15%-20%.
  • Seibert said South America can turn quickly, and South America industry sales are guided down 15%-20%.
  • Seibert said C&F carries four to five months of orders on hand versus the typical two to three, which is a little more than wanted.
  • Seibert cited input and commodity volatility, Black Sea shipment disruptions and softness in the E.U. and Brazil.

  • Dropped topics 'Used and new inventory management' and 'Segment diversification': Kovar and Norwood discussed inventory health and diversification at length in Q3.
